🔬Pathology: Mechanisms, Morphology & Board Distractors

Twelve subject plates spanning basic pathology (cell injury, inflammation, hemodynamics) through developmental/neoplastic, immune, environmental, infectious, and organ-system pathology — every entity framed around etiology → gross → microscopic → clinicopathologic correlation, the core PLE reasoning pattern.

🔬I
Regional block · TOS weight: 8 items / 8% of Pathology PLE; emphasis is predominantly application-level recognition

Compressed Rapid-Review

8% of Pathology PLE

▪Cell Injury / Necrosis

EntityOne-line gross descriptorOne-line microscopic hallmarkConfirming stain / markerSingle distinction from closest mimicAssociated lab
Reversible cell injuryUsually subtle; organ may be pale, swollen, heavyCellular swelling, membrane blebs, ER dilation; nucleus preservedH&E; no specific confirmatory stainvs necrosis: membrane and nucleus remain sufficiently intact for recoveryUsually no specific serum marker
Coagulative necrosisPale, firm infarct, often wedge-shapedPreserved ghost architecture with eosinophilic anucleate cellsH&Evs liquefactive necrosis: architecture remains recognizableTissue-specific enzymes/proteins may rise
Liquefactive necrosisSoft, liquefied tissue; pus when suppurativeComplete enzymatic digestion with loss of architectureH&Evs coagulative: architecture completely lostDepends on cause; leukocytosis if infection
Caseous necrosis“Cheesy,” friable yellow-white materialAmorphous granular necrotic debris within granulomatous inflammationAFB stain for mycobacteria; GMS/PAS if fungal cause consideredvs suppurative necrosis: granulomatous rim rather than neutrophil-dominant abscessCause-specific microbiology
Fat necrosisChalky-white foci in peripancreatic fatGhost adipocytes + basophilic calcium depositsCalcium salts may be demonstrated histochemically SUPPLEMENTARYvs ordinary adipose inflammation: saponification/necrotic adipocytesLipase ↑ in acute pancreatitis; calcium may ↓
Fibrinoid necrosisUsually no specific gross lesionBright eosinophilic vessel-wall materialH&E usually sufficientvs bland vascular hyalinization: inflammatory/necrotizing vessel injuryDepends on associated vasculitis
Gangrenous necrosisDry black ischemic limb; wet if infectedCoagulative necrosis ± superimposed liquefactionH&Evs simple infarct: gangrene is a clinical-gross pattern involving an extremity/bowelDepends on ischemia/infection
Board trap: caseous necrosis strongly suggests TB but is not pathognomonic; some fungal infections may produce similar necrotizing granulomas.
  • DΦCHARACTERISTIC Pyknosis → karyorrhexis → karyolysis = nuclear changes of necrosis.
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not pathognomonic] Increased cytoplasmic eosinophilia + nuclear disappearance supports irreversible cell injury.
  • DPBoard trigger: heart/kidney/spleen infarct → coagulative necrosis; brain infarct → liquefactive necrosis.

▪Apoptosis

FeatureRapid review
GrossUsually no visible gross lesion because isolated cells or small clusters are eliminated
MicroscopyCell shrinkage + intensely eosinophilic cytoplasm + chromatin condensation + apoptotic bodies
Marker/stainCleaved caspase-3 or TUNEL may demonstrate apoptosis SUPPLEMENTARY; routine recognition is primarily on H&E
Closest mimicNecrosis
Single distinctionApoptosis = shrinkage + intact membrane-bound fragments + no significant adjacent inflammation; necrosis = swelling + membrane disruption + inflammation
Lab abnormalityUsually none, because contents are not freely released into extracellular fluid
Tumor markerN/A in this Basic section
Board trap: nuclear fragmentation occurs in both processes; use cell size, membrane integrity, and inflammation to distinguish them.
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not pathognomonic] Apoptotic bodies are membrane-bound fragments containing condensed nuclear/cytoplasmic material.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Absence of adjacent inflammation is one of the most useful slide-level discriminators from necrosis.
  • DΦBoard trigger: solitary shrunken eosinophilic cell with condensed nucleus → apoptosis.

▪Necrosis vs Apoptosis — Must Know

FindingNecrosisApoptosis
Cell sizeSwollenShrunken
Nuclear changePyknosis → karyorrhexis → karyolysisCondensation and fragmentation
MembraneDisruptedEssentially intact around fragments
ContentsLeak outPackaged into apoptotic bodies
InflammationFrequentAbsent/minimal
Clinical contextUsually pathologicPhysiologic or pathologic
  • This contrast is directly summarized in Robbins' necrosis-versus-apoptosis table.

▪Inflammation

PatternGross descriptorMicroscopic hallmarkStain / markerMimic discriminatorLab correlation
Serous inflammationClear, watery fluid; blister/effusionCell-poor proteinaceous fluidH&Evs suppurative: lacks dense neutrophils/pusUsually nonspecific
Fibrinous inflammationShaggy fibrin-coated surfaceEosinophilic fibrin depositionH&Evs fibrosis: fibrin is acute/exudative, not mature collagenInflammatory markers may ↑
Suppurative inflammation / abscessCreamy yellow-white pusDense neutrophils + liquefactive debrisH&E; organism stains/culture as indicatedvs granuloma: neutrophils, not epithelioid macrophagesNeutrophilia, CRP ↑
UlcerCrater-like surface defectSurface necrosis + acute/chronic inflammation + repair at baseH&Evs mass: true loss of surface tissueCause dependent
Chronic inflammationMay be firm, scarred, distortedMacrophages + lymphocytes + plasma cells, tissue destruction and repairH&Evs acute: mononuclear rather than neutrophil-dominant infiltrateCRP/ESR may ↑
Granulomatous inflammationFirm nodules depending on causeEpithelioid histiocytes ± multinucleated giant cellsAFB, GMS/PAS according to suspected causevs granulation tissue: histiocyte aggregates rather than capillary/fibroblast proliferationEtiology-specific
Board trap: granuloma ≠ granulation tissue.
  • Robbins recognizes serous, fibrinous, purulent/suppurative inflammation with abscess formation, and ulcers as major morphologic patterns of acute inflammation.
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not pathognomonic] Neutrophils favor acute inflammation; macrophage/lymphocyte/plasma-cell predominance favors chronic inflammation.
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not pathognomonic] A granuloma is an organized collection of activated epithelioid macrophages; granulomas have infectious and noninfectious causes.

▪Cell and Tissue Regeneration / Repair

EntityGross descriptorMicroscopic hallmarkConfirming stain / markerClosest mimic / distinctionLab
Granulation tissuePink, soft, granular tissue in wound bedNumerous delicate thin-walled capillaries + proliferating fibroblasts in loose ECMTrichrome shows evolving collagenvs granuloma: capillaries + fibroblasts, not epithelioid histiocytesNone specific
Mature scarPale, firm, relatively avascularDense collagen with few vessels/cellsTrichrome highlights collagenvs early granulation tissue: dense collagen and low vascularityNone
Primary intentionThin linear scarMinimal granulation tissue and narrow collagen scarH&E/trichromevs secondary intention: much smaller defect/scarNone
Secondary intentionLarge contracted scarMore granulation tissue, inflammation, collagen, contractionH&E/trichromeProminent wound contractionNone
Hypertrophic scarRaised scar within original woundExcess collagenTrichrome SUPPLEMENTARYvs keloid: does not extend beyond woundNone
KeloidRaised scar extends beyond original woundExcessive dense collagenTrichrome SUPPLEMENTARYExtension beyond wound marginsNone
  • CHARACTERISTIC Granulation tissue = new vessels + fibroblasts + loose ECM ± macrophages.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Mature scar = dense collagen with only scattered vascular channels; Robbins demonstrates this contrast with trichrome stain.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Secondary healing forms more granulation tissue, more scar, and more wound contraction than primary healing.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Keloid extends beyond the original wound and does not regress; hypertrophic scar remains restricted to it.

▪Hypersensitivity Reactions

TypeGross / clinical patternMicroscopic hallmarkDiagnostic test / markerKey discriminatorLab
Type IEdema, erythema, mucus; systemic anaphylaxis possibleImmediate vascular dilation/edema; late phase rich in eosinophils and other leukocytesAllergen-specific IgE; tryptase SUPPLEMENTARYImmediate reaction + eosinophil-rich late phaseIgE may ↑; tryptase may ↑ after systemic mast-cell activation
Type IIOrgan-specific injuryCell lysis/phagocytosis or antibody-mediated inflammationDisease-specific antibodies; direct Coombs in autoimmune hemolysisAntibody directed against cell/tissue antigenDisease specific
Type IIIVasculitic/purpuric or glomerular patternNecrotizing inflammation with fibrinoid necrosisImmune deposits; complement studiesDeposited immune complexesC3/C4 may ↓
Type IVDelayed skin reaction or granulomatous lesionPerivascular mononuclear cells, edema, granulomas, cell destructionPatch/skin testing in appropriate diseasesT-cell-mediated; delayedNo universal serum marker
Board trap: granuloma formation is a possible Type IV pattern, but granulomatous inflammation itself is not diagnostic of hypersensitivity.
  • Robbins' hypersensitivity table links Type I with vascular dilation/edema, Type II with antibody-mediated cellular/tissue injury, Type III with necrotizing vasculitis/fibrinoid necrosis, and Type IV with cellular infiltrates/granuloma formation.
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not pathognomonic] Eosinophil-rich late-phase inflammation supports Type I hypersensitivity.
  • SUGGESTIVE Fibrinoid necrosis supports immune-complex vascular injury but is not unique to Type III reactions.

▪Hemodynamic Disorders

EntityGross descriptorMicroscopic hallmarkStain / markerClosest mimic / distinctionLab
EdemaSwollen tissue; pitting edema if dependentExtracellular fluid separating tissue elementsH&Evs inflammation: edema may lack cellular exudateCause-specific
Pulmonary edemaHeavy, wet lungs; frothy fluid on sectionPale eosinophilic fluid in alveoliH&Evs pneumonia: fluid without dense neutrophilic alveolar infiltrateHypoxemia
HyperemiaBright red tissueDilated vessels filled with oxygenated bloodH&Evs congestion: active increased inflowNone specific
CongestionDusky red-blue/cyanotic tissueVenous/capillary engorgement ± hypoxic injuryH&EPassive impaired outflowCause-specific
Chronic pulmonary congestionFirm brown-red lungSeptal fibrosis + hemosiderin-laden macrophages (“heart failure cells”)Prussian blue highlights hemosiderinvs acute congestion: hemosiderin macrophages/fibrosis indicate chronicityHeart-failure-associated findings
Chronic hepatic congestion“Nutmeg liver”Centrilobular congestion/hemorrhage ± hepatocyte necrosisH&Evs fatty liver: red-brown depressed centrilobular zonesAminotransferases may transiently ↑
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not pathognomonic] Nutmeg liver strongly supports chronic passive hepatic congestion.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Heart failure cells indicate chronic pulmonary congestion/hemorrhage but are not exclusive to heart failure.
  • Board discriminator: hyperemia = active arterial inflow; congestion = passive venous outflow impairment.
  • Board discriminator: transudative edema is primarily hydrostatic/oncotic; inflammatory exudate is protein-rich and cellular.

▪Thromboembolic Disease / Infarction

EntityGross descriptorMicroscopic hallmarkConfirming stain / markerClosest mimic / distinctionLab
Antemortem thrombusFirm, adherent intravascular massLines of Zahn—alternating pale platelet/fibrin and darker RBC layersH&Evs postmortem clot: wall attachment + laminationsD-dimer may ↑
Postmortem clotSoft, gelatinous, nonadherent; red dependent portion + yellow “chicken fat” upper portionNo true lines of ZahnH&ENonadherent and nonlaminatedN/A
Pulmonary saddle embolusLarge embolus straddling pulmonary arterial bifurcationThromboembolic material within pulmonary arteryH&Evs postmortem clot: lines of Zahn/supporting attachment morphologyD-dimer useful as screening in appropriate patients
White infarctPale wedge-shaped lesionCoagulative necrosisH&Evs red infarct: minimal hemorrhageOrgan-specific marker
Red infarctRed-blue/hemorrhagic wedgeIschemic necrosis + marked RBC extravasationH&Evs white infarct: hemorrhagic lesionOrgan-specific marker
Pulmonary infarctRaised red-blue peripheral wedge, apex toward hilumIschemic necrosis of alveolar walls, bronchioles, vessels + hemorrhageH&Evs simple pulmonary hemorrhage: ischemic necrosis is presentMay have D-dimer ↑ if thromboembolic
Board trap: embolus without infarction is possible because bronchial arterial flow can preserve pulmonary tissue.
  • [CHARACTERISTIC, not absolutely pathognomonic] Lines of Zahn strongly establish that a thrombus formed in flowing blood before death.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Pulmonary infarction is usually hemorrhagic and wedge-shaped, with the apex directed toward the hilum.
  • SUGGESTIVE Elevated D-dimer supports active fibrin formation/degradation but is nonspecific; a normal result can help exclude PE in selected low-to-moderate-risk patients.

▪Shock

EntityGross descriptorMicroscopic hallmarkStain / markerClosest mimic / distinctionLab
Hypovolemic shockNo single diagnostic gross lesion; pale/cool periphery clinicallyMultiorgan ischemic injury if prolongedH&Evs isolated ischemia: systemic multiorgan distributionLactate ↑, creatinine ↑ with renal injury
Cardiogenic shockCongested organs may accompany pump failureIschemic multiorgan injuryH&Evs hypovolemic shock: clinical evidence of primary pump failureLactate ↑; cause-specific cardiac markers
Septic shockOften no single defining gross lesion; early skin may be warm/flushedMicrovascular dysfunction ± DIC-associated microthrombi; organ injury may be surprisingly subtleH&Evs simple bacteremia: systemic hypotension/organ dysfunctionLactate ↑; inflammatory markers; coagulation abnormalities if DIC
Shock kidneySwollen kidney may be presentAcute tubular injuryH&Evs chronic kidney disease: acute tubular epithelial injury without chronic scarring patternCreatinine ↑, oliguria
Shock lung / diffuse alveolar damageHeavy, wet lungsEdema and hyaline membranesH&Evs cardiogenic edema: hyaline membranes indicate alveolar-capillary injuryHypoxemia
Shock liverMottled/congested liverCentrilobular ischemic necrosisH&Evs viral hepatitis: zonal centrilobular distribution with hypoperfusionAST/ALT ↑
  • [SUGGESTIVE, not pathognomonic] Hyaline membranes identify diffuse alveolar damage but do not specify shock as its cause.
  • CHARACTERISTIC Centrilobular hepatic necrosis supports severe hypoperfusion because zone 3 is most vulnerable to reduced oxygen delivery.
  • SUGGESTIVE Lactate elevation indicates tissue hypoperfusion but is not specific for shock.
  • Septic shock involves endothelial activation, increased permeability, vasodilation, microvascular thrombosis/DIC, decreased perfusion, and eventual multiorgan failure.

▪Ultra-Compressed Morphology Grid

Disease/processGross → MicroConfirm / labMimic killer
Reversible injurySwollen/pale → hydropic swellingH&E; no specific labIntact nucleus/membrane
Coagulative necrosisPale/firm → ghost architectureH&E; organ marker ↑Architecture preserved
Liquefactive necrosisSoft/liquid → architecture destroyedH&EComplete digestion
Caseous necrosisCheesy → amorphous debris + granulomaAFB/GMS as indicatedGranulomatous rim
Fat necrosisChalky white → ghost fat + calcium soapsLipase ↑ in pancreatitisSaponification
ApoptosisUsually invisible → shrunken apoptotic bodiesCleaved caspase-3/TUNEL SUPPLEMENTARYNo inflammation
AbscessPus → neutrophils + liquefactionCBC: neutrophilia; cultureNeutrophil-dominant
GranulomaFirm nodule → epithelioid histiocytes ± giant cellsAFB/GMS as indicatedHistiocytes, not fibroblasts
Granulation tissuePink, soft, granular → capillaries + fibroblastsTrichromeNew vessels + fibroblasts
Chronic pulmonary congestionBrown-red lung → heart failure cellsPrussian blueHemosiderin macrophages
Hepatic congestionNutmeg liver → centrilobular congestionAST/ALT may ↑Centrilobular pattern
ThrombusAdherent intravascular mass → lines of ZahnD-dimer may ↑Attachment + lamination
Pulmonary infarctRed-blue wedge → hemorrhagic coagulative necrosisD-dimer may ↑Necrosis + hemorrhage
ShockNo unique gross pattern → multiorgan ischemic injuryLactate ↑Systemic distribution

▪Pathognomonic vs Suggestive — Board Safety Check

FindingStrengthWhat it actually means
Apoptotic bodiesCharacteristicStrongly supports apoptosis; not disease-specific
Caseous necrosisSuggestiveStrongly suggests TB in the right setting; not pathognomonic
GranulomaSuggestiveIndicates granulomatous inflammation; does not establish TB
Fibrinoid necrosisSuggestiveVascular immune/severe injury pattern; not disease-specific
Nutmeg liverCharacteristicChronic passive hepatic congestion
Heart failure cellsCharacteristicChronic pulmonary hemorrhage/congestion
Lines of ZahnHighly characteristicAntemortem thrombosis in flowing blood
Hyaline membranesCharacteristicDiffuse alveolar damage; cause must still be identified
Wedge-shaped infarctCharacteristicVascular occlusion pattern; not independently etiologic
Neutrophilic pusCharacteristicSuppurative inflammation/abscess
Epithelioid histiocytesCharacteristicGranulomatous inflammation
No single Basic finding abovePathognomonicMost must be interpreted with site + clinical setting + ancillary tests

▪Final 15-Second Board Recall

  • Swollen cell → reversible injury/necrosis pathway; shrunken cell → apoptosis.
  • Ghost architecture → coagulative necrosis.
  • Brain + liquefaction → liquefactive necrosis.
  • Cheesy → caseous necrosis; chalky white → fat necrosis.
  • Pus + neutrophils → suppurative inflammation.
  • Epithelioid histiocytes → granuloma.
  • Capillaries + fibroblasts → granulation tissue.
  • Heart failure cells + Prussian blue → chronic pulmonary congestion.
  • Nutmeg liver → chronic passive hepatic congestion.
  • Lines of Zahn → antemortem thrombus.
  • Red-blue peripheral wedge in lung → hemorrhagic pulmonary infarct.
  • Type I → IgE/eosinophilic late phase; II → antibody to cell/tissue; III → immune complex/fibrinoid necrosis; IV → T cells/granulomas.
  • Lactate ↑ + multiorgan hypoperfusion → shock.
  • Hyaline membranes → diffuse alveolar damage, not uniquely septic shock.

Developmental Disorders and Neoplasia

8% of Pathology PLE

▪Mendelian Disorders

Marfan Syndrome

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Tall habitus, arachnodactyly, ectopia lentis, dilated aortic root ± aortic dissection
MicroscopyCHARACTERISTIC Cystic medial degeneration with fragmentation/loss of elastic fibers in the aortic media
Confirming stain/markerElastin stain highlights disrupted elastic lamellae; FBN1 testing CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerCystic medial degeneration alone is nonspecific → Marfan phenotype + FBN1 abnormality distinguishes
Lab/tumor markerNo serum marker; genetic testing is diagnostic
Pathognomonic?No — aortic medial degeneration is characteristic, not pathognomonic

Familial Hypercholesterolemia

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Tendon xanthomas + markedly premature atherosclerotic plaques
MicroscopyFoam macrophages; atheroma with cholesterol clefts and fibrous cap
Confirming stain/markerLipid stains on frozen sections may highlight lipid; diagnosis relies on lipid profile/genetic evaluation
Mimic killerOrdinary hyperlipidemia → extreme LDL elevation + tendon xanthomas + premature disease
Lab/tumor markerLDL cholesterol markedly ↑
Pathognomonic?Tendon xanthomas are characteristic, not pathognomonic

Tay-Sachs Disease

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Cherry-red macular spot with pale surrounding retina
MicroscopyBallooned/vacuolated neurons; EM shows concentric whorled/onion-skin lysosomes
Confirming stain/markerHexosaminidase A activity markedly ↓/absent CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerOther storage diseases with cherry-red spot → Hexosaminidase A deficiency
Lab/tumor markerHexosaminidase A ↓/absent
Pathognomonic?No — cherry-red macula occurs in other disorders

▪Chromosomal Disorders

Down Syndrome — Trisomy 21

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Flat facial profile, epicanthic folds, single transverse palmar crease ± congenital cardiac defect
MicroscopyNo syndrome-specific H&E hallmark
Confirming stain/markerKaryotype: 47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21 CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerOther congenital syndromes → extra chromosome 21 on cytogenetics
Lab/tumor markerTrisomy 21; translocation or mosaic forms may occur
Pathognomonic?External phenotype is characteristic, not pathognomonic
  • Robbins specifically associates trisomy 21 with flat facial profile, epicanthic folds, cardiac malformations, leukemia risk, and premature Alzheimer disease.

Patau Syndrome — Trisomy 13

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Polydactyly, microcephaly/microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate ± cardiac/renal defects
MicroscopyNo specific routine histologic hallmark
Confirming stain/markerKaryotype: trisomy 13 CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerEdwards syndrome → polydactyly/craniofacial pattern + chromosome 13 trisomy
Lab/tumor marker47,XX,+13 or 47,XY,+13
Pathognomonic?Gross anomalies are suggestive/characteristic only

Edwards Syndrome — Trisomy 18

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Clenched hands with overlapping fingers, micrognathia, prominent occiput, rocker-bottom feet
MicroscopyNo specific H&E hallmark
Confirming stain/markerKaryotype: trisomy 18 CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerPatau → overlapping-finger pattern + chromosome 18 trisomy
Lab/tumor marker47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18
Pathognomonic?Physical findings are not pathognomonic

22q11.2 Deletion Syndrome

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Thymic/parathyroid hypoplasia + conotruncal cardiac abnormality
MicroscopyReduced/absent thymic architecture; no unique cellular lesion
Confirming stain/markerFISH/copy-number analysis demonstrating 22q11.2 deletion CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerIsolated T-cell immunodeficiency → cardiac defect + hypocalcemia + 22q11.2 deletion
Lab/tumor markerCa²⁺ ↓, T-cell numbers/function ↓
Pathognomonic?Clinical triad is characteristic; deletion testing confirms
  • Robbins links 22q11.2 deletion with cardiac, facial, thymic, and parathyroid abnormalities, including hypocalcemia and diminished T-cell immunity.

Klinefelter Syndrome — 47,XXY

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Small atrophic testes, tall habitus ± gynecomastia
MicroscopyAtrophic, hyalinized seminiferous tubules with absent/reduced spermatogenesis
Confirming stain/markerKaryotype: 47,XXY CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerOther primary testicular failure → XXY karyotype
Lab/tumor markerFSH/LH ↑; testosterone ↓/low-normal
Pathognomonic?Tubular hyalinization is characteristic, not unique

Turner Syndrome — 45,X Spectrum

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Streak ovaries, short stature, webbed neck, broad chest ± coarctation
MicroscopyFibrous gonadal streaks with absent/depleted follicles
Confirming stain/markerKaryotype: 45,X or mosaic/structural X abnormality CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerOther gonadal dysgenesis → X-chromosome abnormality
Lab/tumor markerFSH/LH ↑ from primary ovarian failure
Neoplasia linkY-chromosome material → increased gonadoblastoma risk
Pathognomonic?Streak ovaries are characteristic, not pathognomonic
  • Robbins defines Turner syndrome as complete or partial monosomy X with hypogonadism in phenotypic females.

▪Single-Gene Disorders with Nonclassic Inheritance

Fragile X Syndrome

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Macro-orchidism, long face, prominent jaw, large ears
MicroscopyNo specific diagnostic H&E lesion
Confirming stain/markerFMR1 CGG-repeat testing CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerOther inherited intellectual-disability syndromes → FMR1 repeat expansion
Lab/tumor markerExpanded CGG repeat; no serum marker
Pathognomonic?Macro-orchidism is characteristic, not pathognomonic

Leber Hereditary Optic Neuropathy

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossOptic atrophy may develop; no unique gross lesion
MicroscopyLoss of retinal ganglion cells/optic axons; nonspecific
Confirming stain/markerMitochondrial DNA testing CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerAcquired optic neuritis → maternal inheritance + mtDNA abnormality
Lab/tumor markerNo characteristic serum marker
Pathognomonic?Maternal pedigree is characteristic; morphology is not

Prader-Willi Syndrome

Rapid-review elementFinding
Gross/phenotypeCHARACTERISTIC Obesity, short stature, small hands/feet, hypotonia, hypogonadism
MicroscopyNo specific H&E hallmark
Confirming stain/markerMethylation testing ± deletion analysis CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerAngelman → paternal-expression abnormality = Prader-Willi
Lab/tumor markerAbnormal imprinting/methylation pattern
Pathognomonic?Phenotype alone is not pathognomonic

Angelman Syndrome

Rapid-review elementFinding
Gross/phenotypeSevere neurodevelopmental phenotype ± microcephaly
MicroscopyNo routine diagnostic histologic lesion
Confirming stain/markerMethylation/UBE3A-region analysis CONFIRMATORY
Mimic killerPrader-Willi → maternal-expression abnormality = Angelman
Lab/tumor markerAbnormal imprinting/methylation pattern
Pathognomonic?Clinical findings are characteristic only
  • Robbins places Fragile X, mitochondrial disorders, Prader-Willi, Angelman, and gonadal mosaicism under single-gene disorders with nonclassic inheritance.

▪Benign Neoplasm

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Well circumscribed, expansile, often encapsulated, relatively homogeneous cut surface
MicroscopyWell differentiated cells resembling tissue of origin; pushing border; few normal mitoses
Confirming stain/markerNo universal “benign” stain; lineage IHC identifies origin only
Mimic killerLow-grade malignancy → absence of destructive invasion and metastasis
Tumor marker/labNo universal marker
Benign criterionNo metastasis; no destructive invasion
Pathognomonic?Encapsulation is suggestive, not definitive

▪Malignant Neoplasm

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossCHARACTERISTIC Irregular, infiltrative, poorly demarcated, often with hemorrhage and necrosis
MicroscopyAnaplasia, pleomorphism, hyperchromasia, increased N:C ratio, atypical mitoses ± stromal/vascular invasion
Confirming stain/markerNo universal cancer IHC; use morphology plus lineage-specific markers
Mimic killerBenign neoplasm → destructive invasion or metastasis
Tumor marker/labTumor-specific only
Malignant criterionInvasion proves malignant transformation in appropriate tumors; metastasis definitively identifies malignancy
Pathognomonic?Metastasis = definitive malignant behavior
  • Rubin states that malignancy is proved by invasion in appropriate settings and that the presence of metastases identifies a tumor as malignant.

▪Benign vs Malignant — Ultra-High-Yield

CriterionBENIGNMALIGNANT
DifferentiationUsually well differentiatedMay be poorly differentiated/anaplastic
BorderPushing/circumscribedInfiltrative/destructive
CapsuleOften presentUsually absent
MitosesFew, normalMore frequent; may be atypical
PleomorphismMinimalOften prominent
Necrosis/hemorrhageLess typicalMore common
Local invasionAbsentPresent
MetastasisNeverMay occur
Definitive criterion—Metastasis
Size aloneNot diagnosticNot diagnostic
Growth rate aloneNot diagnosticNot diagnostic
Board trap: a well-differentiated tumor can still be malignant if it invades or metastasizes.
  • DEFINITIVE MALIGNANCY Metastasis.
  • STRONGLY DIAGNOSTIC Destructive local invasion.
  • SUGGESTIVE ONLY Pleomorphism, rapid growth, necrosis, large size, lack of capsule.

▪Carcinoma in Situ

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossOften grossly inapparent or only subtly abnormal
MicroscopyFull-thickness epithelial atypia/dysplasia with no stromal invasion
Confirming stain/markerRoutine H&E demonstrating confinement above basement membrane
Mimic killerInvasive carcinoma → malignant cells breach basement membrane and enter stroma
Tumor marker/labNo universal marker
Malignant statusPreinvasive epithelial malignancy; no access to stromal lymphatics while confined
Pathognomonic discriminatorAbsence vs presence of stromal invasion is the decisive histologic distinction

▪Invasive Carcinoma

Rapid-review elementFinding
GrossIrregular, firm, infiltrative/stellate mass ± ulceration, necrosis, hemorrhage
MicroscopyMalignant glands/nests/cords or cells infiltrating stroma, often with desmoplasia
Confirming stain/markerH&E establishes invasion; cytokeratins support epithelial lineage
Mimic killerCIS → stromal invasion present
Tumor marker/labOrgan-specific
Malignant criterionBasement-membrane breach/stromal invasion

▪Grade vs Stage

FeatureGRADESTAGE
What appears on pathology reportDifferentiation, architecture, mitotic activityAnatomic extent
Core questionWhat does it look like?How far has it spread?
Low categoryWell differentiatedLimited/local disease
High categoryPoorly differentiated/anaplasticMore extensive/nodal/distant disease
Common systemTumor-specific gradingTNM
T—Primary tumor
N—Regional lymph nodes
M—Distant metastasis
Prognostic importanceImportantGenerally more important overall
Board trap: atypical mitoses change grade, not stage.
Board trap: distant metastasis changes stage, regardless of bland microscopic appearance.
  • Grade = microscopic appearance.
  • Stage = extent of disease.

▪Tumor Markers — Lab-Report Review

MarkerAssociated tumor(s) in RobbinsBoard useLimitation

  • Robbins emphasizes that serum tumor proteins such as PSA are useful for monitoring recurrence but are problematic as stand-alone screening tests because of inadequate sensitivity and specificity.
  • Robbins' selected marker table specifically includes hCG, calcitonin, AFP, CEA, immunoglobulins, PSA/PSMA, CA-125, CA19-9, and CA15-3.
  • Rubin likewise emphasizes that no tumor marker unequivocally distinguishes benign from malignant cells.
