▪Final 20-Second Board Recall

  • Marfan → arachnodactyly + ectopia lentis + cystic medial degeneration → characteristic, not pathognomonic.
  • Tay-Sachs → cherry-red spot + ballooned neurons + whorled lysosomes → confirm Hexosaminidase A deficiency.
  • Down = +21; Patau = +13; Edwards = +18.
  • 22q11.2 deletion → cardiac outflow defect + thymic hypoplasia + Ca²⁺ ↓.
  • Klinefelter → small testes + hyalinized seminiferous tubules + FSH/LH ↑ → 47,XXY.
  • Turner → streak ovaries + FSH/LH ↑ → 45,X spectrum.
  • Fragile X → macro-orchidism → confirm FMR1 repeat expansion.
  • Prader-Willi = paternal-expression loss; Angelman = maternal-expression loss.
  • Benign = circumscribed + differentiated + noninvasive + never metastatic.
  • Malignant = invasive ± anaplastic ± metastatic.
  • Metastasis = definitive malignant behavior.
  • CIS = atypia without stromal invasion; invasive carcinoma = basement-membrane breach with tumor in stroma.
  • Grade = microscope; Stage = spread.
  • Tumor markers support and monitor disease; they do not replace morphology/biopsy.
Plate II — Developmental Disorders and NeoplasiaPathology Rapid Review · PLE Board
🛡️III
Regional block · Hypersensitivity disorders; autoimmune diseases; congenital immunodeficiency syndromes; acquired…

Immunopathology

7% of Pathology PLE

▪Hypersensitivity Disorders

Type I — Immediate Hypersensitivity

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Type I hypersensitivity occurs in a previously sensitized individual when allergen cross-links IgE bound to high-affinity FcεRI receptors on tissue mast cells, producing rapid mast-cell activation.
  • The immediate reaction occurs within minutes and is dominated by mast-cell mediators causing vasodilation, increased vascular permeability, smooth-muscle contraction, and mucus secretion.
  • Histamine causes vascular leakage, bronchial smooth-muscle contraction, and mucus secretion; leukotrienes augment bronchospasm and permeability, while cytokines recruit additional inflammatory cells.
  • A late-phase reaction develops roughly 2–24 hours later and contains eosinophils, neutrophils, monocytes, and CD4⁺ T cells; tissue damage, particularly epithelial injury, may then become prominent.
  • Eosinophils contribute to tissue injury by releasing major basic protein and eosinophil cationic protein; persistent allergic injury can therefore evolve from a functional vascular/smooth-muscle event into structural tissue damage.
  • Robbins' hypersensitivity classification identifies anaphylaxis, allergy, and atopic bronchial asthma as prototypes of Type I injury.
Gross morphology
  • Systemic anaphylaxis has no single pathognomonic gross lesion; organs may appear congested and edematous because of generalized vascular permeability.
  • Laryngeal edema may produce a swollen, pale-to-congested mucosa that narrows the airway and causes life-threatening upper-airway obstruction.
  • Atopic bronchial disease may show hyperinflated lungs with bronchi and bronchioles plugged by thick mucus; this appearance is disease-context dependent rather than specific for Type I hypersensitivity.
  • Allergic rhinitis produces swollen, edematous nasal mucosa with increased secretions rather than destructive ulcerative disease.
  • Urticaria produces raised edematous wheals because superficial dermal vascular permeability causes localized fluid accumulation.
  • Classic gross descriptor: “edema + mucus + smooth-muscle spasm” identifies the immediate-phase tissue response but is suggestive, not pathognomonic.
Microscopic morphology
  • The immediate phase shows dilated congested vessels and interstitial edema, with relatively little inflammatory infiltrate initially.
  • The late phase demonstrates an infiltrate rich in eosinophils, with variable neutrophils and T lymphocytes; eosinophil-rich inflammation is a classic board clue for allergic disease.
  • Mast cells contain cytoplasmic granules rich in proteoglycans and mediators; toluidine blue demonstrates metachromatic mast-cell granules.
  • In allergic asthma, mucus plugs may contain Charcot-Leyden crystals, formed from eosinophil-derived galectin-10; these are characteristic but not pathognomonic of asthma.
  • Persistent allergic asthma may show mucus-gland/goblet-cell hyperplasia, airway smooth-muscle hypertrophy, and basement-membrane-region thickening; these represent chronic airway remodeling rather than the acute Type I reaction itself.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none; an eosinophil-rich late-phase infiltrate + mast-cell/IgE clinical context is strongly supportive.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦSystemic vasodilation and increased permeability → reduced effective circulating volume → hypotension and potentially anaphylactic shock.
  • DPBronchial smooth-muscle contraction + mucosal edema + mucus hypersecretion → airflow obstruction → wheezing and dyspnea.
  • Serum tryptase SUPPLEMENTARY may rise soon after significant systemic mast-cell degranulation; a normal delayed sample does not exclude anaphylaxis because timing affects sensitivity.
  • Allergen-specific IgE SUPPLEMENTARY supports sensitization but does not independently prove that a patient's symptoms are caused by that allergen.
  • Peripheral eosinophilia can support an allergic phenotype but is nonspecific and may also accompany parasites, drugs, and other eosinophilic disorders.
  • Closest mimic: non-IgE-mediated anaphylactoid-type mast-cell activation; the morphology can be identical, so demonstration of allergen-specific IgE and the clinical exposure pattern, rather than histology, is the key distinction.

Type II — Antibody-Mediated Hypersensitivity

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Type II hypersensitivity is mediated principally by IgG or IgM directed against antigens on cells, extracellular matrix, or functional receptors.
  • Antibody-coated cells may undergo opsonization and phagocytosis through Fc/complement receptors or may be lysed through complement activation.
  • DΦTissue-bound antibodies can activate complement and recruit neutrophils/monocytes → enzymatic and oxidant injury → inflammation and necrosis.
  • Some Type II disorders produce functional abnormality without significant structural destruction, because antibodies stimulate or inhibit receptors rather than killing cells.
  • Robbins lists autoimmune hemolytic anemia and Goodpasture syndrome as prototypical Type II diseases.
  • DPThe sequence in destructive Type II disease is antibody binding → complement/Fc-receptor activation → inflammatory or cytolytic injury → loss of target-cell/tissue function.
Gross morphology
  • There is no universal gross lesion because the gross appearance depends on the target antigen and organ.
  • In anti-GBM disease, kidneys with severe crescentic glomerulonephritis may become enlarged and pale with cortical petechial hemorrhages.
  • Goodpasture pulmonary involvement can produce heavy, red-brown hemorrhagic lungs because antibodies target alveolar basement membranes as well as the GBM.
  • Autoimmune hemolytic anemia may produce splenomegaly when extravascular removal of antibody-coated erythrocytes is prominent.
  • Receptor-directed Type II diseases may show little gross injury despite profound organ dysfunction; this is particularly important as a board distractor.
  • Classic descriptor: “linear GBM immunofluorescence” belongs to anti-GBM disease and is far more diagnostically useful than gross morphology.
Microscopic morphology
  • Anti-GBM glomerular injury may produce segmental glomerular necrosis and crescent formation due to severe capillary-wall disruption.
  • Immunofluorescence shows smooth linear deposition of IgG ± C3 along the glomerular basement membrane; this is the classic microscopic discriminator from immune-complex disease.
  • Pulmonary involvement shows alveolar hemorrhage and may eventually produce hemosiderin-laden macrophages if bleeding is recurrent.
  • Autoimmune cytopenias do not have a single diagnostic tissue pattern; peripheral smear/bone marrow and antibody testing are more useful than biopsy morphology.
  • Direct antiglobulin (Coombs) testing detects antibody/complement bound to erythrocytes in autoimmune hemolytic anemia.
  • Pathognomonic in the appropriate clinicopathologic setting: linear anti-GBM IgG staining strongly establishes anti-GBM antibody disease; granular deposits instead favor Type III immune-complex injury.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦGlomerular capillary-wall injury → leakage of RBCs and protein into urine → hematuria, RBC casts, proteinuria, and declining GFR.
  • DΦSimultaneous alveolar basement-membrane injury → pulmonary capillary hemorrhage → hemoptysis and falling hemoglobin; kidney + lung involvement is the classic Goodpasture presentation.
  • Circulating anti-GBM antibodies support and confirm the immunologic mechanism in the correct morphologic setting.
  • DΦAutoimmune erythrocyte destruction → hemoglobin ↓, reticulocytes ↑, indirect bilirubin ↑, LDH ↑, and haptoglobin ↓ SUPPLEMENTARY depending on the degree/type of hemolysis.
  • Untreated severe crescentic anti-GBM nephritis can rapidly progress to renal failure because crescents compress the glomerular tuft and obliterate urinary space.
  • Closest mimic: immune-complex crescentic GN; linear IF = anti-GBM, whereas granular IF = immune-complex disease, and pauci-immune disease shows little or no staining.

Type III — Immune-Complex Hypersensitivity

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Type III hypersensitivity results when circulating or locally formed antigen-antibody complexes deposit in vessels, glomeruli, joints, or other tissues.
  • Deposited complexes activate complement; C3a/C5a-mediated inflammation recruits neutrophils, while Fc-receptor engagement further activates leukocytes.
  • DΦNeutrophils release lysosomal enzymes and reactive oxygen species → vascular-wall destruction → necrotizing vasculitis.
  • Platelet activation and thrombosis may compound ischemic injury, particularly in severe localized immune-complex reactions.
  • Systemic serum sickness, SLE, selected glomerulonephritides, and the Arthus reaction are classic Type III prototypes.
  • Complement consumption during active disease may reduce serum C3, making complement levels useful as a laboratory correlate of active immune-complex injury.
Gross morphology
  • Small-vessel immune-complex vasculitis may produce petechiae, palpable purpura, hemorrhage, edema, or focal tissue necrosis.
  • An Arthus reaction produces a localized erythematous/edematous lesion that may progress to focal hemorrhage and cutaneous necrosis.
  • Renal immune-complex disease may produce enlarged swollen kidneys in severe proliferative nephritis, but gross findings are not specific.
  • Serosal immune-complex inflammation may generate fibrinous exudates or effusions depending on the disease.
  • Joint involvement produces swollen tender joints from synovial inflammation and effusion rather than a unique gross lesion.
  • Classic gross descriptor: no pathognomonic gross appearance; palpable purpura/necrotic small-vessel lesion is suggestive of immune-complex vasculitis.
Microscopic morphology
  • The principal lesion is acute necrotizing vasculitis with intense neutrophilic infiltration and fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls.
  • The eosinophilic “smudgy” vessel-wall material represents necrotic tissue admixed with immune complexes, complement, and plasma proteins.
  • In glomeruli, IF demonstrates granular/lumpy deposits of immunoglobulin and complement, contrasting with the smooth linear staining of anti-GBM disease.
  • Electron microscopy demonstrates electron-dense immune deposits at locations determined by the particular immune-complex nephropathy.
  • Neutrophilic nuclear fragmentation may produce leukocytoclasia in small-vessel vasculitis.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none across all Type III diseases; granular immune deposits + appropriate inflammatory lesion are characteristic of immune-complex disease.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPVessel-wall destruction → RBC extravasation → palpable purpura; deeper ischemia/necrosis may produce ulcers and organ dysfunction.
  • DΦGlomerular immune-complex deposition → capillary-wall/mesangial inflammation → hematuria, proteinuria, RBC casts, edema, and renal dysfunction.
  • Serum C3 may fall during active immune-complex disease because complement is consumed; recovery of complement can parallel resolution in some transient diseases.
  • Acute serum sickness may resolve when circulating antigen disappears and immune complexes are catabolized; persistent antigen/autoantigen exposure may instead cause chronic injury.
  • Thrombosis superimposed on an Arthus-type vasculitis worsens local ischemia and necrosis.
  • Closest mimic: Type II anti-GBM injury; the single most useful distinction is granular IF in Type III versus linear IF in anti-GBM Type II disease.

Type IV — T-Cell–Mediated Hypersensitivity

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Type IV hypersensitivity is mediated by T lymphocytes rather than antibodies; CD4⁺ Th1/Th17 cells drive inflammatory injury and CD8⁺ cytotoxic T cells can directly kill target cells.
  • Th1-derived IFN-γ activates macrophages; Th17 cytokines recruit neutrophils and monocytes, producing chronic inflammatory tissue injury.
  • Delayed-type hypersensitivity becomes clinically detectable roughly 24–48 hours after antigen exposure, distinguishing it from immediate IgE-mediated reactions.
  • Persistent antigen can cause prolonged macrophage activation and granuloma formation, particularly when the offending antigen is difficult to eradicate.
  • CD8⁺ cells produce target-cell apoptosis/necrosis in selected autoimmune and virus-associated diseases.
  • Robbins lists contact dermatitis, multiple sclerosis, type 1 diabetes, and tuberculosis as examples of Type IV-type pathology.
Gross morphology
  • Contact dermatitis produces erythematous, swollen, sometimes vesicular skin rather than the transient wheal typical of urticaria.
  • Persistent granulomatous reactions may produce firm, discrete or confluent nodules depending on the affected organ.
  • Type 1 diabetes produces no diagnostic gross pancreatic lesion in routine clinical material; endocrine loss is primarily microscopic and functional.
  • Multiple sclerosis produces sharply demarcated plaques in white matter, but this belongs more directly to the Nervous System TOS section.
  • Tuberculosis produces caseating granulomas, but infection-specific morphology belongs primarily to the Infectious Diseases TOS section.
  • Classic gross clue: delayed indurated skin response rather than immediate edema supports T-cell-mediated hypersensitivity.
Microscopic morphology
  • Delayed reactions show perivascular mononuclear infiltrates, edema, and activated macrophages.
  • Persistent Th1 responses may form granulomas composed of epithelioid macrophages ± multinucleated giant cells surrounded by lymphocytes.
  • Contact dermatitis may show epidermal injury with inflammatory cells and vesicle formation.
  • Cytotoxic T-cell injury causes apoptosis/destruction of target cells without antibody deposition.
  • CD3 highlights T lymphocytes; CD4 and CD8 can define subsets when needed, but routine diagnosis often depends on morphology and clinical context.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none; T-cell-rich delayed inflammation/granulomas are characteristic but occur in many etiologies.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦCytokine-mediated vascular permeability and cellular recruitment → erythematous indurated skin lesion hours after exposure rather than minutes.
  • DPMacrophage accumulation and fibrosis around persistent antigen → tissue distortion and impaired organ function.
  • Skin patch testing can reproduce delayed contact hypersensitivity in selected allergic contact disorders SUPPLEMENTARY.
  • Tuberculin-type testing demonstrates a delayed indurated response reflecting memory T-cell activity but cannot alone prove active infection.
  • Chronic T-cell-mediated diseases persist because recurrent antigen recognition continually recruits and activates macrophages and other inflammatory cells.
  • Closest mimic: Type I hypersensitivity; minutes + edema/mast cells/eosinophils = Type I, while 24–48 hours + T cells/macrophages = Type IV.

▪Autoimmune Diseases

Systemic Lupus Erythematosus

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is a multisystem autoimmune disease characterized by numerous autoantibodies, especially antinuclear antibodies, with much tissue injury mediated by immune complexes.
  • Loss of self-tolerance permits B- and T-cell responses to nuclear antigens; increased apoptotic antigen burden and impaired clearance help sustain autoantibody production.
  • Most systemic lesions reflect Type III immune-complex deposition, particularly in kidneys, small vessels, skin, and serosal surfaces.
  • Antibodies can also directly injure blood cells, producing hemolytic anemia, leukopenia, lymphopenia, or thrombocytopenia through antibody-mediated mechanisms.
  • DΦPersistent immune activation produces a relapsing-remitting pattern, with repeated inflammation → tissue necrosis → repair/scarring → progressive organ dysfunction.
  • Robbins considers anti-double-stranded DNA and anti-Sm antibodies virtually diagnostic/highly specific in the correct clinical setting.
Gross morphology
  • Skin commonly shows the erythematous “butterfly” malar rash across cheeks and nasal bridge; gross appearance is characteristic but not pathognomonic.
  • Serosal surfaces may be roughened and coated by fibrin in acute serositis; chronic lesions may become thickened, opaque, and fibrotic.
  • Libman-Sacks endocarditis produces small sterile warty vegetations, classically on either surface of valve leaflets rather than only along the line of closure.
  • Kidneys can be enlarged and swollen in severe active nephritis but may become granular and contracted in advanced chronic scarring.
  • Joints are painful/swollen because of synovitis but generally lack the grossly destructive erosions typical of long-standing rheumatoid arthritis.
  • Classic gross buzzwords: “butterfly rash” and “Libman-Sacks endocarditis.”
Microscopic morphology
  • Lupus nephritis may show mesangial proliferation, focal/diffuse proliferative lesions, crescents, capillary necrosis, or membranous change depending on class.
  • Class IV diffuse lupus nephritis may display prominent “wire-loop” lesions, caused by extensive subendothelial immune-complex deposits.
  • PAS highlights thickened wire-loop capillary walls; IF shows granular immunoglobulin and complement deposits, while EM demonstrates electron-dense immune deposits.
  • Cutaneous lupus shows vacuolar/basal-cell degeneration, dermoepidermal edema, perivascular inflammation, and sometimes fibrinoid vasculitis; IF may show immunoglobulin/complement at the dermoepidermal junction.
  • Libman-Sacks vegetations are sterile and associated with valve inflammation rather than microorganisms.
  • Pathognomonic feature: no single H&E finding; wire loops are characteristic of severe lupus nephritis but not by themselves absolutely pathognomonic.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦSubendothelial glomerular immune complexes → intense proliferation/inflammation → hematuria, RBC casts, proteinuria, hypertension, and declining renal function.
  • DPClass V subepithelial deposits → diffuse capillary-wall thickening → heavy proteinuria/nephrotic syndrome.
  • ANA is sensitive but nonspecific; anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm are much more specific, while C3/C4/CH50 may decrease during active immune-complex disease.
  • Antiphospholipid antibodies can produce thrombosis, pregnancy morbidity, and laboratory abnormalities such as lupus anticoagulant positivity despite a prothrombotic clinical state.
  • Direct Coombs positivity may identify anti-RBC antibodies; leukopenia, lymphopenia, thrombocytopenia, and hemolytic anemia reflect hematologic immune injury.
  • Closest morphologic mimic for lupus nephritis: other immune-complex GN; systemic SLE phenotype + lupus serology + characteristic mixed granular immune deposits distinguishes lupus.

Rheumatoid Arthritis

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) is a chronic autoimmune inflammatory arthritis driven by T cells, B cells, autoantibodies, macrophages, synovial cells, and inflammatory cytokines.
  • Autoantibodies to citrullinated proteins/peptides (ACPA/anti-CCP) are diagnostically important; rheumatoid factor consists mainly of antibodies reactive with IgG Fc.
  • DΦPersistent synovial inflammation → synoviocyte proliferation, angiogenesis, inflammatory-cell recruitment, fibroblast activation, and osteoclast stimulation.
  • DΦHyperplastic inflamed synovium expands over cartilage to form a pannus → enzymatic cartilage destruction → subchondral bone erosion.
  • DΦContinued injury may bridge opposing bones with fibrous tissue → fibrous ankylosis → possible bony ankylosis.
  • Extra-articular immune injury may produce rheumatoid nodules, vasculitis, pulmonary disease, and serositis.
Gross morphology
  • Synovium is edematous, thickened, hyperplastic, and villous, replacing its normally smooth surface with delicate bulbous projections.
  • Advanced joints demonstrate marginal erosions, cartilage loss, deformity, and eventually fibrous or bony ankylosis.
  • Rheumatoid nodules are firm, nontender, round-to-oval subcutaneous masses, classically near the elbows/forearms and pressure points.
  • Chronic joint destruction causes characteristic deformity because pannus erodes cartilage and supporting structures.
  • The lesion is typically symmetric and favors small joints of the hands and feet before larger joints.
  • Classic gross buzzword: “pannus”—inflamed proliferative synovial tissue growing over and eroding articular cartilage.
Microscopic morphology
  • Synovium shows synovial-cell hyperplasia, dense CD4⁺ T-cell/B-cell/plasma-cell/macrophage infiltrates, increased vascularity, and surface fibrinopurulent exudate.
  • Lymphoid aggregates may form follicle-like structures within the subsynovium.
  • Pannus comprises edematous proliferative synovium, inflammatory cells, granulation tissue, and fibroblasts extending over articular cartilage.
  • Osteoclast activity erodes subchondral bone and contributes to marginal erosions and cysts.
  • A rheumatoid nodule has central fibrinoid necrosis surrounded by palisading activated macrophages, with peripheral lymphocytes/plasma cells.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none; a palisading rheumatoid nodule is highly characteristic but can be mimicked by other necrobiotic granulomatous lesions.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPPannus erodes cartilage → joint-space loss → pain and impaired motion; subchondral erosion and ligament/tendon injury → progressive deformity.
  • Anti-CCP/ACPA is more diagnostically specific than rheumatoid factor; rheumatoid factor is common but may occur in other diseases and some individuals without RA.
  • ESR/CRP commonly rise with active inflammation but are nonspecific and do not establish diagnosis.
  • Rheumatoid nodules and vasculitis generally indicate more systemic/severe disease rather than isolated mild synovitis.
  • Chronic inflammatory burden and loss of joint architecture can cause irreversible disability even when active inflammation later decreases.
  • Closest morphologic mimic: osteoarthritis; RA = proliferative inflammatory pannus and marginal erosions, whereas OA shows cartilage fibrillation, osteophytes, subchondral sclerosis, and little primary synovial inflammation.

Sjögren Syndrome

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Sjögren syndrome is a chronic autoimmune disease targeting lacrimal and salivary glands, producing keratoconjunctivitis sicca and xerostomia.
  • DΦPredominantly CD4⁺ T cells together with B cells and plasma cells infiltrate exocrine glands → progressive epithelial injury → acinar atrophy → fibrosis.
  • Periductal/perivascular inflammation begins focally and progressively expands; B-cell follicles and germinal centers may develop.
  • Ductal epithelial hyperplasia can narrow/obstruct ducts, while progressive acinar loss directly reduces salivary/tear production.
  • Chronic B-cell stimulation creates a background in which monoclonal populations and B-cell lymphoma may eventually arise.
  • Anti-SSA/Ro and anti-SSB/La are important serologic markers; ANA and rheumatoid factor are also frequently present.
Gross morphology
  • Parotid and sometimes submandibular glands may be unilaterally or bilaterally enlarged, while lobulation can initially remain preserved.
  • With advanced disease, glands become atrophic and may show fatty replacement and fibrosis rather than persistent enlargement.
  • Oral mucosa is dry and may demonstrate fissuring, ulceration, dental disease, and reduced salivary pooling.
  • Corneal/conjunctival surfaces become dry and irritated because deficient lacrimal secretion damages the surface epithelium.
  • Parotid enlargement in an autoimmune patient is characteristic but must not automatically be assumed benign because lymphoma risk is increased.
  • Classic gross descriptor: “sicca syndrome” — dry eyes + dry mouth.
Microscopic morphology
  • Earliest lesion: periductal and perivascular lymphocytic infiltration of salivary/lacrimal tissue.
  • Progressive disease shows extensive lymphocytes and plasma cells, often with lymphoid follicles and germinal centers.
  • Ductal epithelial hyperplasia develops while acini undergo atrophy, fibrosis, hyalinization, and eventually fatty replacement.
  • Rubin describes proliferating myoepithelial/ductal structures surrounded by lymphocytes as epimyoepithelial islands / lymphoepithelial sialadenitis.
  • Dense inflammation may mimic lymphoma; preservation of a polyclonal/reactive pattern supports autoimmune sialadenitis.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none; focal lymphocytic sialadenitis in the appropriate sicca/serologic setting is diagnostically supportive.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPAcinar destruction → salivary flow ↓ → xerostomia → dysphagia for dry foods, dental caries, oral fissuring, and impaired taste.
  • DΦLacrimal-gland destruction → tears ↓ → corneal epithelial dryness → burning, itching, keratitis, erosion, and potential ulceration.
  • Anti-SSA/Ro and anti-SSB/La support the diagnosis; rheumatoid factor and ANA are common but less disease-specific.
  • Persistent parotid enlargement or emergence of a dominant glandular mass raises concern for B-cell/MALT-type lymphoma, requiring tissue evaluation for clonality and destructive growth.
  • Unlike SLE, primary glomerular immune-complex disease is uncommon; renal manifestations more often involve tubular dysfunction.
  • Closest mimic: salivary gland lymphoma; reactive polymorphic lymphoid infiltrate with residual glandular architecture favors Sjögren, while a monotonous clonal infiltrate with destructive expansion favors lymphoma.

Systemic Sclerosis — Scleroderma

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Systemic sclerosis is characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular dysfunction/injury, and immune abnormalities affecting skin and multiple internal organs.
  • Early microvascular endothelial injury and perivascular inflammation precede progressive luminal narrowing and tissue ischemia.
  • DΦFibroblast activation produces excessive extracellular matrix/collagen → progressive replacement and entrapment of normal tissue structures.
  • Chronic ischemia plus fibrosis causes cutaneous atrophy, gastrointestinal hypomotility, pulmonary fibrosis, myocardial dysfunction, and renal vascular disease.
  • Early inflammatory lesions may contain CD4⁺ T cells, but late disease is dominated morphologically by dense fibrosis and vascular narrowing.
  • Anti-Scl-70/topoisomerase I is strongly associated with diffuse systemic sclerosis; anticentromere antibodies are associated with limited/CREST-pattern disease.
Gross morphology
  • Skin becomes thick, taut, shiny, and tightly bound to underlying structures; fingers may become tapered and claw-like with restricted movement.
  • Progressive ischemia can cause fingertip ulceration, resorption of terminal phalanges, and occasionally autoamputation.
  • Face may become mask-like because dense dermal/subcutaneous collagen limits normal movement.
  • Esophageal fibrosis produces a rigid, poorly motile lower esophagus described as “rubber-hose–like” in the reference.
  • Pulmonary fibrosis produces firm, contracted lungs in advanced disease; renal disease is principally vascular rather than a specific gross mass lesion.
  • Classic gross descriptors: “sclerodactyly,” “mask-like face,” “claw-like fingers,” and calcinosis in CREST-pattern disease.
Microscopic morphology
  • Skin shows progressive dense compact collagen deposition in the dermis, often accompanied by epidermal thinning and loss of rete pegs.
  • Dermal appendages such as hair follicles and adnexal glands become markedly atrophic or disappear as collagen replaces surrounding tissue.
  • Small vessels demonstrate basement-membrane thickening, endothelial injury, partial luminal occlusion, and later hyaline thickening.
  • Esophagus shows atrophy and collagenous replacement of muscularis, particularly in the lower two-thirds.
  • Masson trichrome SUPPLEMENTARY can highlight increased collagen but is not required for diagnosis.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none; widespread dermal fibrosis + vascular change in the characteristic clinical/serologic setting is strongly diagnostic.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦDermal/subcutaneous fibrosis → loss of skin elasticity → fixed fingers, reduced joint mobility, and mask-like facies.
  • DPSmall-vessel vasculopathy → episodic digital ischemia → Raynaud phenomenon; sustained ischemia → ulcers, infarction, and tissue loss.
  • DΦEsophageal muscular fibrosis → hypomotility and lower-sphincter dysfunction → dysphagia, reflux, strictures, and possible Barrett esophagus.
  • DPInterstitial pulmonary fibrosis → restrictive physiology and hypoxemia; pulmonary disease is an important determinant of mortality.
  • Anti-Scl-70 supports diffuse disease/pulmonary fibrosis risk; anticentromere antibody supports limited/CREST disease and generally a more limited cutaneous pattern.
  • Closest mimic: SLE/dermatomyositis skin disease; dense progressive dermal collagen with appendage loss and marked vasculopathy favors systemic sclerosis.

▪Congenital / Primary Immunodeficiency Syndromes

Severe Combined Immunodeficiency — SCID

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • SCID comprises genetically heterogeneous disorders sharing profound impairment of both cell-mediated and humoral immune responses.
  • Many forms primarily disrupt T-cell development; because normal antibody responses depend on T-cell help, humoral immunity becomes secondarily ineffective.
  • X-linked SCID commonly affects signaling through the common cytokine-receptor γ-chain; ADA-deficient SCID causes toxic metabolite accumulation and lymphocyte injury.
  • DPFailure of effective T- and B-cell immunity → inability to control common and opportunistic pathogens → recurrent disseminated infection.
  • Severe infection produces progressive tissue injury, wasting, malabsorption, and failure to thrive; without immune reconstitution, disease is often fatal early in life.
  • Robbins emphasizes Candida, Pneumocystis, Pseudomonas, CMV, varicella, and diverse bacterial infections as characteristic threats.
Gross morphology
  • The thymus is small/hypoplastic, reflecting failure of normal lymphoid development.
  • Peripheral lymphoid tissues and lymph nodes are also hypoplastic, and tonsils/lymphoid aggregates may be poorly developed.
  • Infants may show oral thrush, chronic skin/diaper inflammation, and severe wasting rather than one characteristic internal gross lesion.
  • Pulmonary and gastrointestinal organs may exhibit infection-specific gross lesions secondary to opportunistic pathogens.
  • The gross absence of normally prominent lymphoid tissue in a severely infected infant is a major morphologic clue.
  • Classic gross descriptor: small thymus + poorly developed lymphoid tissue.
Microscopic morphology
  • In common forms the thymus is small and markedly depleted of lymphocytes.
  • X-linked SCID may show thymic lobules composed predominantly of undifferentiated epithelial cells resembling fetal thymus.
  • ADA-deficient SCID may retain remnants of Hassall corpuscles, but lymphoid elements remain severely depleted.
  • Lymph nodes/spleen demonstrate marked depletion of T-cell zones and, in some cases, B-cell zones as well.
  • CD3/CD4/CD8 and B-cell markers SUPPLEMENTARY on tissue or flow cytometry can document lymphocyte-lineage depletion.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none; severe lymphoid depletion plus immunophenotype/genetic defect establishes the syndrome.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPT-cell failure → severe viral/fungal/protozoal infection; impaired B-cell function → recurrent bacterial infection, producing a broad pathogen spectrum.
  • DPChronic GI infection → diarrhea/malabsorption → failure to thrive.
  • Marked lymphopenia, abnormal lymphocyte subsets, and very poor immunoglobulin/vaccine responses SUPPLEMENTARY support the diagnosis.
  • Opportunistic infection in infancy, particularly thrush with failure to thrive and profound lymphopenia, should trigger evaluation for SCID.
  • Hematopoietic stem-cell transplantation can restore immune function; prognosis without definitive immune reconstitution is poor.
  • Closest mimic: advanced pediatric HIV; onset from infancy + congenital lymphocyte-development abnormality/genetic testing favors SCID.

X-Linked Agammaglobulinemia — Bruton Disease

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) results from failure of pre-B cells to mature into functional B cells.
  • DΦMature circulating B cells become absent or markedly reduced → plasma cells fail to develop → all immunoglobulin classes become profoundly decreased.
  • DPAntibody deficiency impairs opsonization and neutralization → recurrent infection with extracellular encapsulated bacteria and selected enteroviruses/Giardia.
  • Maternal Ig initially protects infants; recurrent infections classically begin after approximately 6 months, when maternal antibody levels decline.
  • T-cell-mediated immunity is relatively preserved, explaining why many intracellular/fungal infections are less prominent than in combined deficiencies.
  • Robbins identifies absent/markedly reduced B cells, low immunoglobulins, underdeveloped germinal centers, and absent plasma cells as the classic pathology.
Gross morphology
  • Tonsils, adenoids, lymph nodes, and Peyer patches may be small or poorly developed because germinal-center B-cell compartments are deficient.
  • Recurrent sinopulmonary infections can cause chronic bronchial wall injury and bronchiectasis over time.
  • No characteristic tumor-like or destructive gross lesion directly identifies XLA.
  • Lymphoid organs lack the reactive enlargement expected in an immunocompetent child exposed to recurrent antigen.
  • GI infections may produce chronic mucosal inflammation secondary to Giardia/enteric infection.
  • Classic gross clue: a boy with recurrent bacterial infections but scant tonsillar/lymph-node tissue.
Microscopic morphology
  • Germinal centers in lymph nodes, tonsils, Peyer patches, and appendix are underdeveloped or absent.
  • Plasma cells are absent throughout the body, a particularly high-yield morphologic clue.
  • Bone marrow contains pre-B cells but peripheral tissues lack mature B-cell populations.
  • T-cell zones are relatively preserved because T-cell immunity is intact.
  • Flow cytometry SUPPLEMENTARY shows severely reduced/absent mature B-cell markers such as CD19/CD20 in peripheral blood.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none by H&E alone; absent mature B cells + pan-hypogammaglobulinemia establishes the immune phenotype.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦLack of antibody-mediated opsonization → recurrent S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, and S. aureus sinopulmonary infections.
  • DΦLack of neutralizing antibody → increased vulnerability to enteroviruses; Giardia can cause persistent intestinal infection.
  • DΦSerum IgG, IgA, IgM and other classes are markedly ↓, while circulating B cells are absent or markedly reduced.
  • Symptoms appearing after maternal Ig wanes at about 6 months are a classic board discriminator from severe combined defects presenting even earlier.
  • Chronic infections can produce irreversible bronchiectasis despite immunoglobulin replacement.
  • Closest mimic: CVID; XLA = very low/absent B cells and early male childhood onset, whereas CVID generally retains B cells but has defective plasma-cell differentiation.

Digeorge Syndrome — Thymic Hypoplasia

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • DiGeorge syndrome represents a T-cell immunodeficiency caused by hypoplasia/aplasia of thymic development and is commonly part of the 22q11.2 deletion syndrome.
  • Developmental abnormalities of the pharyngeal pouch derivatives affect thymus and parathyroids simultaneously.
  • DPThymic hypoplasia → reduced T-cell maturation → impaired cell-mediated immunity.
  • DΦParathyroid hypoplasia → reduced parathyroid function → hypocalcemia.
  • Conotruncal heart and great-vessel defects frequently coexist and can dominate early clinical presentation.
  • Immunologic impairment varies according to the degree of thymic underdevelopment.
Gross morphology
  • The thymus is small or absent at surgery/autopsy rather than normally lobulated and prominent in infancy.
  • Parathyroids may likewise be hypoplastic or absent.
  • Cardiac gross lesions include conotruncal/outflow-tract malformations.
  • Craniofacial/palatal abnormalities may accompany the immune/endocrine lesions.
  • No color or cut-surface descriptor is uniquely diagnostic; organ absence/hypoplasia is the central gross feature.
  • Classic gross triad: thymic hypoplasia + parathyroid hypoplasia + conotruncal cardiac defect.
Microscopic morphology
  • Thymic tissue is markedly reduced and may lack normal cortical thymocyte populations.
  • Normal corticomedullary architecture is diminished in severe disease.
  • Peripheral lymphoid organs may show reduced T-cell-dependent paracortical areas.
  • B-cell follicles may remain because humoral lineage development is not the primary defect.
  • FISH/copy-number testing can demonstrate a 22q11.2 deletion and is more definitive than histology.
  • Pathognomonic feature: no single microscopic feature; 22q11.2 deletion in the appropriate developmental phenotype confirms the syndrome.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPT-cell deficiency → recurrent viral, fungal, and opportunistic infection proportional to thymic impairment.
  • DΦParathyroid hypoplasia → serum calcium ↓ → tetany, neuromuscular irritability, or seizures.
  • Flow cytometry may show reduced T-cell numbers, particularly in severe forms.
  • Congenital heart disease may cause cyanosis, murmurs, heart failure, or require early surgical correction.
  • Immune function may improve with age in partial forms because residual thymic tissue supports some T-cell maturation.
  • Closest mimic: isolated SCID; hypocalcemia + conotruncal heart disease + 22q11.2 deletion strongly favors DiGeorge syndrome.

Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is an X-linked immune disorder classically defined by thrombocytopenia, eczema, and recurrent infection.
  • Immune dysfunction affects both cellular and humoral responses; T-cell numbers progressively decrease in peripheral blood and lymph-node T-cell zones.
  • Antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens are especially poor, contributing to recurrent bacterial infection.
  • Platelet abnormalities produce bleeding manifestations, while immune dysregulation produces eczema and increased autoimmune phenomena.
  • Progressive immune dysfunction permits chronic infection and increases the risk of B-cell lymphoma.
  • The defect is associated with WASP, which is involved in cytoskeleton-dependent signaling and cellular responses.
Gross morphology
  • Petechiae, purpura, and bleeding lesions may be visible grossly because of thrombocytopenia.
  • Eczematous skin lesions provide a characteristic external morphologic component.
  • The thymus may appear morphologically normal early in the disease.
  • Lymph nodes may lose normal paracortical prominence as T-cell depletion progresses.
  • Recurrent infection produces organ-specific suppurative/inflammatory changes rather than a syndrome-specific gross lesion.
  • Classic clinical-gross triad: eczema + thrombocytopenic bleeding + recurrent infections.
Microscopic morphology
  • Early thymus may be normal, but peripheral lymphoid tissue progressively loses T-cell zones/paracortical lymphocytes.
  • Peripheral blood smear shows thrombocytopenia; small platelets SUPPLEMENTARY are a classic diagnostic clue.
  • Eczematous skin shows inflammatory dermatitis but lacks a syndrome-specific microscopic feature.
  • B-cell follicles can persist, distinguishing the disorder from profound agammaglobulinemia.
  • If lymphoma develops, tissue must be evaluated for a clonal B-cell proliferation rather than simply reactive hyperplasia.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none by morphology alone.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPPlatelet deficiency/dysfunction → petechiae, mucosal bleeding, and hemorrhagic complications.
  • DPImmune deficiency → recurrent bacterial and viral infections.
  • DΦSerum IgM tends to be ↓, IgG often normal, while IgA and IgE may be elevated, a useful laboratory pattern in the reference.
  • Progressive T-cell depletion distinguishes Wiskott-Aldrich from purely humoral immunodeficiencies.
  • B-cell lymphoma risk is increased and represents an important long-term neoplastic complication.
  • Closest mimic: isolated immune thrombocytopenia with eczema; combined immune deficiency plus the characteristic immunoglobulin/T-cell abnormalities favors Wiskott-Aldrich.

Chronic Granulomatous Disease — CGD

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • DPChronic granulomatous disease results from inherited defects in phagocyte oxidase → defective respiratory burst → impaired microbial killing.
  • Neutrophils can phagocytose organisms but fail to generate normal oxidative microbicidal activity.
  • Inadequate acute bacterial killing leads to persistent organisms and recurrent inflammation.
  • DPMacrophages accumulate to contain organisms that neutrophils cannot eradicate → chronic macrophage-rich inflammation and granuloma formation, giving the disease its name.
  • Patients are particularly vulnerable to selected bacteria and fungi, including S. aureus and certain gram-negative/fungal organisms.
  • X-linked and autosomal-recessive forms involve different components of the phagocyte oxidase complex.
Gross morphology
  • Recurrent infections produce multiple abscesses and inflammatory masses in lymph nodes, lungs, liver, skin, and other organs.
  • Granulomatous inflammatory masses may cause luminal obstruction in hollow organs.
  • Abscesses may be firm or fluctuant depending on the balance of suppuration and chronic granulomatous reaction.
  • There is no unique color or cut-surface pattern specific for CGD.
  • Chronic inflammation may lead to fibrosis and organ distortion after repeated infections.
  • Classic gross clue: recurrent deep abscesses/granulomatous masses beginning in childhood.
Microscopic morphology
  • Lesions show macrophage-rich chronic granulomatous inflammation around persistent organisms and inflammatory debris.
  • Neutrophilic suppuration may coexist during acute infection.
  • Granulomas themselves are not morphologically specific for CGD and require immunologic/laboratory correlation.
  • Organism-directed stains such as GMS/PAS for fungi or Gram/AFB stains according to suspected organism are important in infected tissue.
  • Dihydrorhodamine flow cytometry SUPPLEMENTARY demonstrates deficient oxidative burst and is the preferred functional confirmation; historical nitroblue tetrazolium testing assesses the same pathway.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none on tissue section; abnormal oxidative-burst testing confirms the functional defect.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPDefective killing → recurrent skin, lung, lymph-node, liver, or bone infections.
  • DPPersistent inflammation → granuloma formation → obstruction or mass effect, adding mechanical organ dysfunction to infection.
  • CBC may show inflammatory leukocytosis during infection but cannot identify the underlying phagocyte defect.
  • Abnormal oxidative-burst assay distinguishes CGD from ordinary recurrent infection with intact neutrophil killing.
  • Repeated infection and fibrosis can lead to chronic organ damage despite structurally normal neutrophil numbers.
  • Closest mimic: myeloperoxidase deficiency; CGD has defective oxidative burst, whereas MPO deficiency retains respiratory burst but loses downstream MPO-dependent killing.

▪Acquired Immunodeficiency

HIV Infection / AIDS

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • HIV infection causes progressive acquired immunodeficiency principally through infection, dysfunction, and depletion of CD4⁺ helper T cells.
  • Viral infection also perturbs macrophages/dendritic cells and causes generalized immune activation, contributing to progressive immune dysregulation.
  • Early disease includes vigorous immune activation and B-cell stimulation; with progression, CD4⁺ loss becomes dominant and immune architecture collapses.
  • Declining cellular immunity permits reactivation/new infection by opportunistic organisms that would ordinarily be controlled.
  • Loss of immune surveillance and chronic B-cell activation increase risks of Kaposi sarcoma, B-cell lymphoma, cervical carcinoma, and anal carcinoma.
  • Progressive immunodeficiency eventually produces severe opportunistic infection, malignancy, CNS disease, wasting, and death if untreated.
Gross morphology
  • Early HIV may cause generalized lymphadenopathy, with enlarged lymph nodes reflecting reactive B-cell follicular hyperplasia.
  • Late AIDS produces small atrophic “burnt-out” lymph nodes rather than persistent hyperplasia.
  • Spleen and thymus also undergo late lymphoid depletion and involution.
  • Opportunistic infections produce organ-specific gross patterns: Pneumocystis pneumonia, mycobacterial disease, fungal lesions, CMV ulcers, CNS toxoplasmosis, etc.
  • Kaposi sarcoma produces red-purple cutaneous/mucosal plaques or nodules and may involve viscera.
  • DΦClassic gross evolution: enlarged reactive nodes early → small “burnt-out” lymphoid organs late.
Microscopic morphology
  • Early lymph nodes show marked B-cell follicular hyperplasia with enlarged irregular/serpiginous follicles and attenuated mantle zones.
  • As disease progresses, follicles regress; lymphocytes are depleted, follicular dendritic-cell networks are disrupted, and germinal centers can become hyalinized.
  • Late nodes are profoundly depleted and may appear nearly empty; opportunistic organisms may fill macrophages despite a surprisingly weak inflammatory response.
  • Severe CD4⁺ deficiency can prevent normal granuloma formation, so mycobacterial infection may appear poorly granulomatous or non-granulomatous.
  • CD4 immunophenotyping/flow cytometry documents depletion, while special organism stains such as GMS, AFB, PAS, or mucicarmine [SUPPLEMENTARY according to pathogen] are often critical because organisms may be abundant with little host response.
  • Pathognomonic feature: no anatomic lesion is diagnostic of HIV itself; Robbins specifically states that AIDS tissue changes are neither specific nor diagnostic.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPCD4 count ↓ progressively with immune failure; severe depletion predicts specific opportunistic infections and is central to staging immune compromise.
  • HIV RNA/viral load reflects active viral replication and is used to follow treatment response [SUPPLEMENTARY current laboratory framing].
  • CD4 <200/µL represents severe cellular immunodeficiency in classic CDC categorization; lower counts are associated with progressively more severe opportunistic disease.
  • CMV retinitis occurs predominantly at very low CD4 counts, often <50/µL; cryptococcosis, toxoplasmosis, PML, atypical mycobacteria, and other opportunistic infections emerge with advanced immune depletion.
  • AIDS-defining neoplasms include Kaposi sarcoma, primary CNS lymphoma, and invasive cervical carcinoma in the referenced classification.
  • Closest mimic: congenital combined immunodeficiency; positive HIV testing + acquired progressive CD4 depletion + characteristic temporal lymph-node evolution favors HIV/AIDS.

Immunodeficiency of Aging — Immunosenescence

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • Aging is associated with progressive decline in immune responsiveness rather than one discrete congenital defect.
  • The thymus undergoes physiologic involution after puberty, reducing the production of naïve T cells with advancing age.
  • Reduced immune reserve weakens responses to newly encountered antigens and contributes to increased vulnerability to infection.
  • Declining cellular immunity also favors reactivation of latent infections such as herpesviruses or tuberculosis in susceptible individuals.
  • Functional responses to vaccines can be reduced even when absolute lymphocyte counts remain within reference ranges.
  • The TOS specifically includes aging as an acquired immunodeficiency state, but the primary source does not specify one required histologic lesion; therefore, morphology should be interpreted as supportive rather than diagnostic.
Gross morphology
  • The aging thymus is small and involuted, with increasing replacement of lymphoid parenchyma by adipose tissue.
  • Normal young thymic lobulation becomes less prominent as cortical lymphoid tissue decreases.
  • Spleen and lymph nodes do not have a single diagnostic senescent gross appearance.
  • Opportunistic/recurrent infections produce organ-specific gross lesions rather than a unique “aging immunodeficiency” pattern.
  • Severe systemic illness can further accelerate thymic involution even in younger persons, making thymic size alone nonspecific.
  • Classic gross descriptor: “involuted fatty thymus” is characteristic of normal aging, not pathognomonic of clinically significant immunodeficiency.
Microscopic morphology
  • Thymic lymphoid cellularity decreases progressively with increasing adipose replacement.
  • Remaining thymic epithelial elements/Hassall corpuscles may persist within involuted tissue.
  • Peripheral lymphoid tissues may demonstrate less robust germinal-center responses, but no single H&E finding diagnoses immunosenescence.
  • Age-related immune dysfunction is primarily functional and cannot be reliably graded from routine tissue sections.
  • CD4/CD8/B-cell counts SUPPLEMENTARY may be clinically measured but do not provide a universal diagnostic pattern of aging.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DΦReduced immune reserve → increased susceptibility to infection and poorer ability to contain newly encountered pathogens.
  • Decreased T-cell responsiveness contributes to reactivation of latent infections in vulnerable older adults.
  • Vaccine antibody responses may be weaker or less durable despite technically adequate vaccination SUPPLEMENTARY.
  • No single serum immunoglobulin or lymphocyte threshold defines physiologic immunosenescence.
  • Comorbid disease, malnutrition, medications, malignancy, and organ dysfunction frequently compound age-related immune decline.
  • Closest mimic: pathologic acquired immunosuppression; aging produces gradual broad decline without one severe lineage-specific defect, whereas drug-induced/HIV-related states can produce more defined immune abnormalities.

Malnutrition-Associated Immunodeficiency

Etiology and pathogenesis
  • The TOS explicitly recognizes malnutrition as a cause of acquired immunodeficiency.
  • Protein/calorie and micronutrient deficiency reduce the substrates required for lymphocyte proliferation, antibody production, epithelial barrier maintenance, and effective innate immune function.
  • Impaired epithelial/mucosal barriers increase microbial entry, while deficient cell-mediated and humoral responses limit pathogen clearance.
  • DPInfection then increases metabolic demand and nutrient loss → worsened malnutrition → further immune impairment, producing a self-reinforcing cycle.
  • SUPPLEMENTARY Severe protein-energy malnutrition may cause prominent thymic/lymphoid atrophy, reflecting loss of lymphoid tissue rather than inflammatory destruction.
  • Unlike HIV or congenital immunodeficiency, no single pathogenetic gene, antibody, or immune-cell marker defines nutritional immunodeficiency.
Gross morphology
  • Severe malnutrition produces generalized wasting and loss of subcutaneous fat/muscle according to the nutritional syndrome.
  • SUPPLEMENTARY The thymus and peripheral lymphoid tissues may become small/atrophic in severe protein-energy malnutrition.
  • Lymph nodes may be inconspicuous because lymphoid responses to antigen are blunted.
  • GI mucosal injury/atrophy may coexist and further worsen nutrient absorption depending on the nutritional state.
  • Superimposed infections may appear unusually extensive relative to the amount of inflammatory response.
  • There is no pathognomonic gross lesion of malnutrition-associated immune failure.
Microscopic morphology
  • SUPPLEMENTARY Lymphoid tissue may show reduced lymphocyte populations and diminished reactive follicular activity in severe malnutrition.
  • Thymic cortical lymphocytes may be depleted, with relative prominence of epithelial/adipose components.
  • Tissue repair can be delayed because collagen synthesis, cell proliferation, and inflammatory responses are nutritionally dependent.
  • Organism burden may be disproportionate to the cellular inflammatory response in severe immune compromise.
  • Routine H&E cannot determine which nutrient deficit is responsible for impaired immunity.
  • Pathognomonic feature: none.
Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
  • DPImpaired cell-mediated immunity → increased infection risk and reduced ability to clear intracellular organisms.
  • DPPoor barrier function + immune deficiency → recurrent respiratory/GI infection, which further increases catabolism and nutrient loss.
  • Serum albumin SUPPLEMENTARY can be reduced in severe protein deficiency but is confounded by inflammation, liver disease, renal loss, and fluid status.
  • CBC may demonstrate anemia, leukopenia, or other nutritional abnormalities but no specific hematologic pattern confirms immune dysfunction.
  • Immune competence can improve substantially when the nutritional deficit is corrected, provided irreversible organ damage or persistent infection has not developed.
  • Closest mimic: primary immunodeficiency; acquired nutritional history + generalized nutritional abnormalities + reversibility with nutritional restoration favor malnutrition.

▪Primary-Source Table / Visual Audit

  • The attached primary source is a five-page PRC Pathology Table of Specifications, not a histology atlas.
  • Its Immunopathology row assigns 7% / 7 items to hypersensitivity disorders, autoimmune diseases, congenital immunodeficiency syndromes, and acquired immunodeficiency from HIV, aging, and malnutrition.
  • The row allocates 2 Remembering, 4 Applying, and 1 Analyzing questions, which justifies prioritizing slide/lab interpretation over isolated immunology definitions.
  • The primary source contains no gross specimen photograph, photomicrograph, or immunofluorescence image to reproduce; its visual content is the TOS table itself.
  • The relevant content-authority figures therefore supply the morphology used for review: Robbins' hypersensitivity table/figures, lupus kidney/skin/valve figures, Sjögren salivary-gland figure, RA pannus/nodule figures, systemic-sclerosis skin image, primary-immunodeficiency development diagram, and HIV lymph-node morphology.
  • These reference visuals are described below as examination-oriented image walkthroughs.

▪Reference Table / Figure Content — Exam-Oriented Description

Robbins Table 6.1 — Mechanisms of Hypersensitivity

  • DΦType I: IgE/mast cells → vascular dilation, edema, smooth-muscle contraction, mucus, late inflammation.
  • DΦType II: IgG/IgM against cell or tissue → phagocytosis/lysis, inflammation, or functional disturbance.
  • DΦType III: immune-complex deposition → complement/leukocytes → inflammation and fibrinoid necrotizing vasculitis.
  • DPType IV: activated T cells → cytokine inflammation/macrophage activation or cytotoxicity → perivascular infiltrate, edema, granuloma, cell destruction.
  • DΦBoard first look: identify the morphology first, then map it to the effector: eosinophils/edema → I; linear fixed-antigen antibody → II; granular complexes/fibrinoid necrosis → III; T cells/granuloma → IV.
  • Robbins' table uses anaphylaxis/asthma, AIHA/Goodpasture, SLE/serum sickness, and contact dermatitis/TB-type responses as prototypes.

Robbins Figure — Immediate Hypersensitivity

  • Immediate phase: look first for vasodilation, congestion, edema.
  • Late phase: look next for eosinophils, neutrophils, and T cells.
  • Timing clue: minutes for immediate vascular/smooth-muscle reaction; hours for inflammatory-cell recruitment.
  • High power: identify eosinophils by bilobed nuclei and coarse eosinophilic cytoplasmic granules.
  • Marker clue: mast cells can demonstrate metachromasia with toluidine blue.
  • DΦBoard first look: “minutes after allergen” + edema → Type I even before the eosinophilic late phase develops.

Lupus Nephritis — Figure 6.26

  • Scanning power: decide whether involvement is focal or diffuse and whether glomeruli are hypercellular.
  • Low power: class IV disease shows marked proliferation involving much of the glomerulus.
  • High power: search capillary walls for wire-loop thickening.
  • PAS: highlights the prominent subendothelial deposit-associated loops.
  • IF: expect granular IgG/complement rather than a linear GBM pattern.
  • EM: identify subendothelial electron-dense deposits corresponding to the wire loops.

Lupus Skin — Figure 6.27

  • Scanning power: inspect the epidermal-dermal interface.
  • Low power: note edema and interface injury.
  • High power: identify vacuolar degeneration of the basal epidermal layer and perivascular inflammation.
  • IF: immunoglobulin deposits may be demonstrated along the dermoepidermal junction.
  • Important trap: the lupus-band-type finding is not absolutely specific for SLE.
  • DΦBoard first look: photosensitive malar rash + basal-vacuolar degeneration + autoimmune serology → SLE.

Libman-Sacks Endocarditis — Figure 6.28

  • Gross first look: identify small warty sterile vegetations on a thickened valve leaflet.
  • Distribution: lesions can occur on either surface of the leaflet.
  • Mimic: rheumatic vegetations classically favor the lines of closure; infective vegetations tend to be larger and destructive.
  • Low power: examine valve tissue for inflammation/fibrinoid injury beneath vegetations.
  • High power: do not expect colonies of microorganisms as in infective endocarditis.
  • Board first look: SLE + small sterile vegetations on either side of valve = Libman-Sacks.

Sjögren Syndrome — Figure 6.29

  • Gross first look: enlarged salivary gland/parotid in active disease.
  • Scanning power: assess preservation of lobules and distribution of lymphoid infiltrates.
  • Low power: identify dense periductal lymphocytic aggregates and possible germinal centers.
  • High power: look for ductal epithelial hyperplasia, plasma cells, and progressive acinar destruction.
  • Late field: expect fibrosis and fatty replacement.
  • DΦBoard first look: gland-destructive lymphoid infiltrate + xerostomia/xerophthalmia + SSA/SSB → Sjögren.

Rheumatoid Arthritis — Figures 26.43 and 26.44

  • Gross first look: thick villous synovium over an eroded joint surface.
  • Scanning power: identify exuberant proliferative synovium extending over cartilage as pannus.
  • Low power: see dense lymphoid aggregates, vascular proliferation, and synovial hyperplasia.
  • High power: identify mixed CD4⁺ T cells, B cells, plasma cells, macrophages, and surface fibrin/inflammation.
  • Rheumatoid nodule: central fibrinoid necrosis surrounded by palisading macrophages.
  • Board first look: erosive inflammatory pannus distinguishes RA from degenerative OA.

Systemic Sclerosis — Skin Figure

  • Scanning power: compare dermal thickness with normal skin.
  • Low power: dense collagen expands the dermis and extends around/replaces normal appendages.
  • High power: identify compact collagen, reduced adnexal structures, and small-vessel thickening.
  • Gross-clinical image: fingers become immobile and claw-like because fibrosis binds skin/subcutaneous tissue.
  • Ischemia: reduced blood supply produces fingertip ulceration.
  • Board first look: dense dermal collagen + appendage loss + Raynaud/sclerodactyly = systemic sclerosis.

Primary Immunodeficiency Development Figure

  • The figure maps blocks in lymphocyte development to selected diseases: SCID, XLA, DiGeorge, hyper-IgM, IgA deficiency, MHC-II deficiency, CVID.
  • DΦBone marrow block in B-cell maturation → XLA.
  • DPThymic/T-cell developmental failure → DiGeorge/selected SCID patterns.
  • DΦCombined pathway disruption → SCID.
  • DPLate B-cell/plasma-cell differentiation impairment → CVID/IgA-type deficiencies.
  • Board first look: determine which lineage is missing before memorizing the genetic defect.
  • This approach correlates the histologic absence of cell compartments with the laboratory immunophenotype.

HIV Lymph Node Evolution

  • Early scanning power: enlarged irregular/serpiginous follicles with preserved overall node enlargement.
  • Early high power: active B-cell follicular proliferation reflects polyclonal B-cell stimulation.
  • Late scanning power: node becomes hypocellular and atrophic with severe loss of lymphoid architecture.
  • Late high power: germinal centers become hyalinized; opportunistic organisms may be abundant within macrophages.
  • Important board clue: profound CD4 depletion may abolish granuloma formation even in mycobacterial disease.
  • Classic phrase: “burnt-out lymph node” = advanced HIV-associated lymphoid involution.

▪Key Morphologic Points

  • DΦType I: edema/congestion immediately → eosinophil-rich infiltrate later; eosinophilia is characteristic, not pathognomonic.
  • Type II anti-GBM: linear IgG ± C3 along GBM; this is the single most important morphology-based discriminator.
  • Type III: granular immune deposits + fibrinoid necrotizing vasculitis.
  • Type IV: T-cell/macrophage inflammation ± granuloma, with delayed rather than immediate onset.
  • Linear IF = fixed basement-membrane antigen; granular IF = deposited immune complexes.
  • SLE: wire-loop glomerular capillaries, granular IF, butterfly rash, Libman-Sacks vegetations.
  • Wire loops are characteristic of severe lupus nephritis, but the diagnosis still requires clinicopathologic/serologic correlation.
  • Lupus-band-type dermoepidermal Ig deposition is not specific for SLE.
  • RA: pannus is the key joint lesion; pannus destroys cartilage and subchondral bone.
  • Rheumatoid nodule: central fibrinoid necrosis + palisading macrophages.
  • RA vs OA: inflammatory pannus/erosions versus cartilage fibrillation, osteophytes, and subchondral sclerosis.
  • DΦSjögren: dense periductal lymphocytes → acinar destruction → fibrosis/fat; epimyoepithelial islands are a classic microscopic phrase.
  • Sjögren vs lymphoma: polymorphous reactive lymphoid tissue versus destructive monoclonal B-cell expansion.
  • Systemic sclerosis: dense dermal collagen + adnexal atrophy + small-vessel disease.
  • Systemic sclerosis vs SLE: fibrosis/vascular narrowing dominates systemic sclerosis; immune-complex inflammation dominates many SLE lesions.
  • XLA: absent/underdeveloped germinal centers + absent plasma cells.
  • SCID: severely hypoplastic lymphoid tissue with profound T-cell ± B-cell depletion.
  • DiGeorge: small/absent thymus with T-cell deficiency and hypocalcemia.
  • Wiskott-Aldrich: progressive loss of lymph-node T-cell zones + thrombocytopenia/eczema.
  • Chédiak-Higashi: giant leukocyte cytoplasmic granules are the classic smear clue.
  • CGD: macrophage-rich granulomas are suggestive; the oxidative-burst assay, not H&E, establishes the functional defect.
  • Early HIV: follicular hyperplasia; late HIV: lymphoid depletion and “burnt-out” nodes.
  • AIDS infection trap: absence of granulomas does not exclude mycobacteria when CD4 cells are profoundly depleted.
  • Pathognomonic language: most immune morphologies are characteristic rather than pathognomonic because multiple immune diseases share common inflammatory patterns.

▪Key Clinical Points

  • DΦIgE/mast-cell activation → vascular leak + smooth-muscle contraction → urticaria/bronchospasm/hypotension; late eosinophilic inflammation sustains symptoms.
  • DΦAnti-GBM antibody → linear basement-membrane attack → glomerular capillary rupture → hematuria/RBC casts; alveolar attack → hemoptysis.
  • DΦImmune complexes → complement activation → neutrophilic vasculitis → fibrinoid necrosis; complement consumption → C3/C4 may fall.
  • DΦT-cell hypersensitivity → delayed macrophage/T-cell inflammation → induration or granuloma rather than immediate wheal formation.
  • DΦSLE immune complexes → wire-loop glomerular injury → proteinuria/hematuria/renal dysfunction; anti-dsDNA/Sm and complement changes support the diagnosis.
  • DΦSLE valve immune injury → sterile Libman-Sacks vegetations; absence of destructive infectious colonies separates it from infective endocarditis.
  • DΦRA synovitis → pannus → cartilage/bone erosion → deformity and ankylosis; anti-CCP/ACPA is the key serologic partner to the morphology.
  • DΦSjögren gland infiltration → acinar loss → tears/saliva ↓ → keratoconjunctivitis sicca + xerostomia; SSA/SSB support the diagnosis.
  • DΦPersistent B-cell activation in Sjögren → increased lymphoma risk; a new dominant glandular mass should not be assumed to be benign autoimmune enlargement.
  • DΦSystemic-sclerosis vascular injury → Raynaud; fibrosis → taut skin, esophageal dysmotility, pulmonary fibrosis, myocardial dysfunction; Scl-70/anticentromere help define phenotype.
  • SCID = broad pathogen vulnerability because both cellular and humoral defenses fail.
  • XLA = extracellular bacterial/enteroviral infection after 6 months because B cells and immunoglobulins are profoundly deficient while T-cell immunity remains relatively intact.
  • DiGeorge = T-cell deficiency + hypocalcemia + conotruncal cardiac lesion because thymus/parathyroids share developmental origins.
  • Wiskott-Aldrich = thrombocytopenia + eczema + recurrent infection; the laboratory pattern is part of the diagnosis because morphology is nonspecific.
  • DΦCGD = neutrophils present but cannot kill normally → recurrent deep infection + granuloma; functional oxidative-burst testing is more useful than counting neutrophils.
  • DΦHIV → early reactive follicular hyperplasia → progressive CD4 depletion → burnt-out lymphoid tissue → opportunistic infection and malignancy.
  • Low CD4 count changes morphology itself: severe infections may contain numerous organisms with a minimal inflammatory or granulomatous response.
  • Aging and malnutrition cause acquired immune decline without one diagnostic histologic lesion, so board questions should be solved through context, infection pattern, lymphoid atrophy, and laboratory/nutritional findings rather than a purported pathognomonic slide.
  • DΦPLE priority: with 4 of 7 Immunopathology items at Applying level, always pair the finding: morphology → immune mechanism → clinical effect → confirmatory lab, e.g. wire loops → immune complexes → nephritis → anti-dsDNA/low complement.
