    LeiomyosarcomaFleshy necrotic mass → atypical mitotic smooth muscleLineage IHCAtypia+mitoses+necrosis
    Serous borderlinePapillary cystic mass → epithelial complexityMorphologyNo destructive invasion
    Serous carcinomaBilateral complex masses → high-grade invasive tumorWT1/PAX8 SUPPStromal invasion

    ▪Final 20-Second Board Recall

    • Postinfectious GN: neutrophilic hypercellularity + granular IgG/C3 + subepithelial humps + low C3.
    • RPGN: crescent = severity; linear = anti-GBM, granular = immune complex, pauci-immune = ANCA.
    • IgA nephropathy: mesangial IgA.
    • MCD: normal LM + negative IF + foot-process effacement.
    • FSGS: focal segmental sclerosis/hyalinosis; beware sampling error.
    • Membranous: GBM spikes + subepithelial deposits + granular IgG/C3.
    • Diabetic nephropathy: PAS-positive Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodules; microalbuminuria appears first.
    • ATI: tubular epithelial injury + muddy-brown casts.
    • Acute pyelo: neutrophilic tubulitis + WBC casts.
    • Chronic pyelo: coarse polar scars + blunted calyces + thyroidization.
    • Drug AIN: drug exposure + eosinophils/lymphocytes + tubulitis.
    • Benign HTN: grain-leather + hyaline arteriolosclerosis.
    • Malignant HTN: flea-bitten + onion-skinning ± fibrinoid necrosis.
    • ADPKD: massive bilateral multicystic kidneys; ARPKD = radial collecting-duct dilations + congenital hepatic fibrosis.
    • Hydronephrosis: dilated pelvis/calyces + thinned cortex.
    • Clear-cell RCC: yellow renal mass + clear cells + delicate vessels + renal-vein invasion.
    • Wilms: tan-gray childhood renal mass + triphasic blastema/epithelium/stroma.
    • BPH: transition-zone nodules + two-layered glands + basal cells preserved.
    • Prostate carcinoma: peripheral small glands + basal-cell loss + perineural invasion + osteoblastic metastases.
    • Seminoma: homogeneous fleshy mass + clear cells + lymphocytic septa; pure seminoma does not raise AFP.
    • Yolk-sac tumor: Schiller-Duval bodies + AFP.
    • Choriocarcinoma: cytotrophoblast + syncytiotrophoblast + hemorrhage + hCG.
    • Endometriosis: chocolate cyst + endometrial glands/stroma ± hemosiderin.
    • Cervical HSIL: p16 block positivity supports lesion, but stromal invasion establishes carcinoma.
    • Leiomyoma: firm gray-white whorled mass + bland smooth muscle.
    • Leiomyosarcoma: atypia + mitoses + tumor-cell necrosis; leiomyoma is not its usual precursor.
    • Ovarian serous tumors: psammoma bodies do not prove malignancy; destructive stromal invasion does.
    • Tumor-marker rule: PSA, AFP, hCG and CA-125 support diagnosis or monitoring in the appropriate context but do not replace morphology; IHC establishes lineage, while invasion/metastasis establishes malignant behavior.
    Plate X — Genito-Urinary DisordersPathology Rapid Review · PLE Board
    ⚖️XI
    Regional block · 6% = 6 items covering disorders of the pancreas, adrenal glands, parathyroid glands, thyroid…

    Endocrine Disorders

    6% of Pathology PLE

    ▪Pituitary Disorders

    Pituitary Adenoma / Pituitary Neuroendocrine Tumor

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossSoft gelatinous sellar mass ± suprasellar extension, sellar enlargement, hemorrhage/necrosis in large lesions
    MicroscopyMonomorphic endocrine cells + sparse/disrupted reticulin network
    Confirming stain/markerReticulin stain demonstrates loss of normal framework; hormone/lineage IHC defines subtype
    Mimic killerPituitary hyperplasia → generally retains acinar reticulin architecture
    LabProlactinoma: prolactin ↑; somatotroph tumor: IGF-1 ↑; corticotroph tumor: ACTH ↑/cortisol ↑
    Pathognomonic?Monomorphism + reticulin loss is highly characteristic, not a stand-alone universal pathognomonic finding
    BehaviorUsually locally behaving; invasion may occur; metastatic pituitary carcinoma is exceptionally rare
    Board trap: mild/moderate prolactin elevation in a large non-prolactin-secreting sellar lesion can result from stalk effect.
    • Robbins emphasizes cellular monomorphism and absence of a significant reticulin network as major morphologic distinctions from normal anterior pituitary.
    • DΦMass-effect clue: macroadenoma → optic-chiasm compression → bitemporal hemianopia.

    Sheehan Syndrome

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossAcute anterior-pituitary infarction → later small fibrotic remnant / empty sella
    MicroscopyIschemic necrosis → loss/fibrosis of adenohypophyseal tissue; posterior lobe relatively spared
    Confirming stain/markerN/A; diagnosis relies on postpartum setting + pituitary hormone deficiencies
    Mimic killerPituitary apoplexy → hemorrhage/infarction usually occurs within a pre-existing adenoma
    LabProlactin ↓, gonadotropins ↓, TSH/T4 ↓, ACTH/cortisol ↓ according to extent
    Pathognomonic?No; postpartum hemorrhagic shock + anterior-pituitary necrosis is the diagnostic context
    • Pregnancy enlarges the anterior pituitary without a proportional blood-supply increase; obstetric hemorrhage/shock can therefore precipitate infarction, while the posterior pituitary is relatively resistant.
    • DPClassic clue: postpartum hemorrhage → failure to lactate → amenorrhea/panhypopituitarism.

    Central Diabetes Insipidus

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossCause dependent; hypothalamic/stalk/posterior-pituitary lesion may be present or gland may appear normal
    MicroscopyNo single diagnostic histologic lesion
    Confirming marker/testFunctional testing; desmopressin response SUPPLEMENTARY
    Mimic killerNephrogenic DI → fails to appropriately concentrate urine after desmopressin
    LabUrine osmolality/specific gravity ↓; serum osmolality/Na⁺ ↑ if water intake insufficient
    Pathognomonic?N/A
    • Robbins distinguishes central ADH deficiency from nephrogenic DI and notes that both produce large volumes of dilute urine with low specific gravity.

    ▪Thyroid Disorders

    Graves Disease

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossDiffuse, symmetric, soft “meaty” thyroid enlargement
    MicroscopyTall crowded follicular epithelium + scalloped colloid + papillary infoldings without fibrovascular cores
    Confirming markerTSH-receptor antibodies; thyroid hormones
    Mimic killerPapillary carcinoma → true fibrovascular papillae + characteristic PTC nuclei
    LabT3/T4 ↑, TSH ↓
    Pathognomonic?Scalloped colloid is characteristic, not pathognomonic
    • Robbins describes diffuse enlargement, a soft meaty cut surface, tall hyperplastic epithelium, papillary projections lacking fibrovascular cores, and pale scalloped colloid.

    Hashimoto Thyroiditis

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossDiffusely enlarged firm, pale yellow-tan gland; later may become atrophic
    MicroscopyDense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate with germinal centers + Hürthle-cell change
    Confirming markerAnti-TPO and anti-thyroglobulin antibodies support diagnosis
    Mimic killerHürthle-cell neoplasm → discrete clonal oncocytic tumor rather than diffuse autoimmune lymphoid background
    LabEstablished disease: T4 ↓, TSH ↑
    Pathognomonic?Hürthle cells are NOT pathognomonic; they occur in several thyroid injuries/neoplasms
    • Robbins characterizes Hashimoto thyroiditis by progressive parenchymal destruction, Hürthle cells, lymphoplasmacytic infiltrates, germinal centers, and variable fibrosis.

    Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorMALIGNANT, generally differentiated
    GrossFirm white-tan thyroid nodule ± cystic change, fibrosis, calcification; may be multifocal/infiltrative
    MicroscopyOrphan Annie-eye nuclei + nuclear grooves + intranuclear pseudoinclusions ± true papillae/psammoma bodies
    Confirming markerMorphologic nuclear pattern; thyroid-lineage IHC SUPPLEMENTARY
    Mimic killerGraves papillary hyperplasia → lacks PTC nuclear features and fibrovascular cores
    Tumor marker/labThyroglobulin SUPPLEMENTARY useful after treatment; not diagnostic for a new nodule
    SpreadClassically lymphatic → cervical lymph nodes
    Pathognomonic?Characteristic nuclear constellation is a diagnostic morphologic pattern; psammoma bodies alone are not specific
    • Robbins states that PTC diagnosis can be made from the characteristic ground-glass/Orphan Annie nuclei, grooves and pseudoinclusions, even without papillary architecture.

    Follicular Thyroid Carcinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorMALIGNANT
    GrossSolitary gray-tan/pink follicular nodule, sometimes apparently encapsulated; widely invasive tumors penetrate thyroid/soft tissue
    MicroscopyFollicular architecture with capsular and/or vascular invasion
    Confirming criterionExtensive histologic sampling of tumor-capsule interface
    Mimic killerFollicular adenoma → completely lacks capsular/vascular invasion
    Tumor marker/labThyroglobulin may monitor differentiated disease SUPPLEMENTARY
    SpreadPredominantly hematogenous → bone/lung
    Definitive malignant criterionCapsular or vascular invasion
    Board trap: FNA can diagnose a follicular neoplasm but cannot reliably prove follicular carcinoma because invasion cannot be assessed cytologically.
    • Robbins explicitly states there is no reliable cytologic distinction between follicular adenoma and minimally invasive carcinoma; the diagnosis requires capsular and/or vascular invasion.

    Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorMALIGNANT neuroendocrine tumor
    GrossFirm pale gray-tan thyroid nodule; hereditary disease often bilateral/multicentric
    MicroscopyNests/trabeculae of neuroendocrine cells with salt-and-pepper chromatin + stromal amyloid
    Confirming stain/markerCalcitonin, chromogranin/synaptophysin; Congo red confirms amyloid
    Mimic killerFollicular-derived carcinoma → lacks calcitonin-positive C-cell phenotype and characteristic amyloid
    Tumor marker/labSerum calcitonin ↑; CEA useful for tumor burden/follow-up
    SpreadCervical nodes → lung/liver/bone
    Pathognomonic?Calcitonin-positive neuroendocrine tumor + stromal amyloid is highly diagnostic
    • Robbins notes calcitonin storage/secretion and identifies CEA as a useful biomarker; amyloid is an important diagnostic feature of medullary carcinoma.

    Anaplastic Thyroid Carcinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorHIGHLY MALIGNANT
    GrossLarge poorly circumscribed hard gray-white mass invading neck structures ± hemorrhage/necrosis
    MicroscopyBizarre spindle and giant cells, severe pleomorphism, numerous mitoses and necrosis
    Confirming markerCytokeratin/EMA may confirm epithelial differentiation
    Mimic killerSarcoma → epithelial-marker positivity supports anaplastic thyroid carcinoma
    Tumor marker/labNo useful serum marker; thyroid function may remain unrelated to tumor burden
    Malignant criterionDestructive local invasion/metastatic behavior
    Pathognomonic?No single histologic feature is unique
    • Rubin describes a large infiltrative gray-white mass with bizarre spindle/giant cells, polyploid nuclei, numerous mitoses, necrosis and frequent cytokeratin/EMA positivity.

    ▪Parathyroid Disorders

    Parathyroid Adenoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorBENIGN
    GrossSolitary, well-circumscribed soft tan to reddish-brown nodule
    MicroscopyUniform chief cells, little stromal fat, often a rim of compressed normal parathyroid
    Confirming markerPTH establishes parathyroid differentiation
    Mimic killerHyperplasia → multigland disease rather than solitary lesion with compressed residual rim
    LabPTH ↑, Ca²⁺ ↑, phosphate ↓
    Pathognomonic?Residual rim is characteristic, not invariably present
    Board trapBizarre endocrine nuclei do not prove malignancy
    • Robbins describes a solitary soft tan-red nodule with uniform chief cells, inconspicuous fat and a peripheral rim of compressed gland; it specifically warns that endocrine atypia is not a criterion for malignancy.

    Primary Parathyroid Hyperplasia

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorBENIGN hyperplastic process
    GrossEnlargement of multiple glands, often all four but frequently asymmetric
    MicroscopyDiffuse/multinodular chief-cell hyperplasia with stromal fat ↓
    Confirming markerPTH + biochemical hyperparathyroidism
    Mimic killerAdenoma → usually one dominant lesion with suppressed/normal remaining glands
    LabPTH ↑, Ca²⁺ ↑, phosphate ↓ in primary disease
    Pathognomonic?No; multigland distribution is the major distinction
    • Robbins emphasizes that primary hyperplasia is classically multiglandular but may be asymmetric and can therefore mimic adenoma.

    Secondary Hyperparathyroidism — CKD

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossMultigland enlargement, often nodular
    MicroscopyDiffuse/multinodular chief-cell proliferation with reduced stromal fat
    Confirming markerBiochemical CKD/PTH pattern
    Mimic killerPrimary adenoma → solitary dominant lesion + hypercalcemia
    LabPTH ↑, phosphate ↑, Ca²⁺ low/normal early in CKD-associated disease
    ComplicationRenal osteodystrophy / osteitis fibrosa
    Pathognomonic?Morphology alone cannot separate primary from secondary hyperplasia

    Parathyroid Carcinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorMALIGNANT
    GrossSingle gray-white, firm irregular/adherent mass, sometimes >10 g
    MicroscopyUniform parathyroid cells in nodular/trabecular architecture ± thick fibrous capsule
    Confirming markerPTH confirms lineage, not malignancy
    Mimic killerAdenoma → lacks destructive invasion/metastasis
    LabOften markedly PTH ↑ and Ca²⁺ ↑
    Definitive malignant criterionInvasion of surrounding tissue or metastasis
    Pathognomonic?Cytologic atypia/fibrous bands alone are not diagnostic of carcinoma
    • Robbins states that cytologic detail is unreliable and that surrounding-tissue invasion and metastasis are the only reliable criteria for parathyroid carcinoma.

    ▪Adrenal Disorders

    Cushing Syndrome — Morphologic Patterns

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossACTH-dependent → bilateral cortical hyperplasia; exogenous steroids → bilateral cortical atrophy; adrenal tumor → unilateral lesion
    MicroscopyPituitary may show Crooke hyaline change; adrenal hyperplasia shows expanded lipid-rich/lipid-poor cortical zones
    Confirming markerCortisol testing + ACTH to classify source
    Mimic killer“Cushing disease” specifically means pituitary ACTH source, not every case of Cushing syndrome
    LabACTH-dependent: cortisol ↑ with ACTH ↑/inappropriately normal; adrenal autonomous: cortisol ↑, ACTH ↓
    Pathognomonic?Crooke change is characteristic of glucocorticoid excess, not source-specific
    • Robbins describes Crooke hyaline change, bilateral cortical atrophy with exogenous glucocorticoids, and bilateral adrenal hyperplasia in ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome.

    Primary Hyperaldosteronism / Conn Adenoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossSmall (<2 cm), solitary, well-circumscribed bright-yellow adrenal cortical nodule
    MicroscopyUniform lipid-laden cortical cells; treated tumors may show spironolactone bodies
    Confirming marker/testAldosterone ↑ with renin suppressed
    Mimic killerSecondary hyperaldosteronism → renin ↑, not suppressed
    LabAldosterone ↑, renin ↓ ± K⁺ ↓, metabolic alkalosis
    Pathognomonic?Spironolactone bodies are characteristic treatment-related inclusions, not mandatory
    • Robbins describes Conn adenomas as small, solitary, bright-yellow tumors and identifies laminated eosinophilic spironolactone bodies after spironolactone treatment.

    Primary Chronic Adrenal Insufficiency — Addison Disease

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossAutoimmune disease → irregularly shrunken adrenal glands
    MicroscopySevere cortical cell loss + lymphoid inflammation in collapsed stroma; medulla relatively preserved
    Confirming marker/testCortisol/ACTH profile
    Mimic killerSecondary adrenal insufficiency → ACTH ↓, no hyperpigmentation, aldosterone relatively preserved
    LabCortisol ↓, ACTH ↑, Na⁺ ↓, K⁺ ↑ in primary disease
    Pathognomonic?Small inflamed adrenal cortex is characteristic of autoimmune adrenalitis but cause requires context
    • Robbins describes autoimmune adrenalitis as irregularly shrunken glands with only scattered residual cortical cells, lymphoid inflammation, and preservation of the medulla.

    Pheochromocytoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorPotentially metastatic neuroendocrine tumor
    GrossSmall lesions yellow-tan; larger tumors may be hemorrhagic, necrotic and cystic
    MicroscopyZellballen nests + granular cells with salt-and-pepper chromatin
    Confirming markerChromogranin + synaptophysin in chief cells; S-100 highlights sustentacular cells
    Mimic killerAdrenocortical tumor → steroidogenic cortical phenotype rather than zellballen/neuroendocrine IHC
    LabPlasma/urine metanephrines ↑
    Malignant criterionHistologic pleomorphism is unreliable; metastatic behavior is decisive in classic board pathology
    Pathognomonic?Zellballen is highly characteristic, not exclusive to adrenal pheochromocytoma
    • Robbins describes the classic zellballen, salt-and-pepper nuclei, chromogranin/synaptophysin-positive chief cells and S-100-positive sustentacular cells, and warns that bizarre cytology does not reliably predict metastasis.

    Adrenocortical Adenoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorBENIGN
    GrossSmall, well-circumscribed yellow/yellow-brown homogeneous nodule
    MicroscopyBland cortical-type cells with lipid-rich vacuolated or eosinophilic cytoplasm; mitoses inconspicuous
    Confirming markerSF-1 / inhibin-α establish cortical lineage
    Mimic killerAdrenocortical carcinoma → large invasive tumor with necrosis, high mitotic activity and aggressive features
    LabHormone levels depend on functionality: cortisol or aldosterone may ↑
    Malignant criterion absentNo destructive invasion/metastasis
    • Robbins notes that benign adenomas may show some endocrine atypia, so nuclear pleomorphism alone should not trigger a carcinoma diagnosis.

    Adrenocortical Carcinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorMALIGNANT
    GrossOften very large, poorly demarcated, variegated mass with hemorrhage, necrosis and cystic change
    MicroscopyIncreased mitoses/atypical mitoses, diffuse growth, necrosis and invasive behavior
    Confirming markerSF-1 confirms adrenocortical lineage; it does not prove malignancy
    Mimic killerAdenoma → small homogeneous circumscribed lesion without major necrosis/invasion
    Tumor marker/labMay secrete cortisol/androgens; no universal serum malignancy marker
    Definitive malignant behaviorLocal invasion and/or metastasis
    Pathognomonic?No single microscopic feature; malignancy is based on integrated morphologic behavior
    • Robbins contrasts the usual small yellow adenoma with the typically large invasive, hemorrhagic-necrotic, variegated carcinoma.

    ▪Endocrine Pancreas

    Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossPancreas usually lacks a diagnostic gross lesion
    MicroscopyEarly insulitis with T lymphocytes + reduction in islet size/number and β-cell mass
    Confirming markerInsulin IHC may demonstrate β-cell depletion SUPPLEMENTARY; islet autoantibodies support autoimmune disease
    Mimic killerType 2 DM → islet amyloid rather than classic active autoimmune insulitis
    LabGlucose/HbA1c ↑; endogenous insulin/C-peptide ↓ as β-cell failure progresses
    Pathognomonic?Insulitis is highly characteristic but may be absent in longstanding disease
    • Robbins describes T1D with small/inconspicuous islets and T-lymphocyte–predominant insulitis, particularly around clinical presentation.

    Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossNo characteristic pancreatic gross lesion
    MicroscopyExtracellular islet amyloid ± modest loss of islet-cell mass/fibrosis
    Confirming stainCongo red confirms amyloid
    Mimic killerType 1 DM → lymphocytic insulitis/β-cell depletion rather than dominant islet amyloid
    LabGlucose/HbA1c ↑; insulin/C-peptide variable depending disease stage
    Pathognomonic?Islet amyloid is characteristic, NOT pathognomonic; it can occur in older nondiabetic individuals
    • Robbins notes that amyloid in T2DM accumulates around capillaries and between islet cells and may eventually almost replace the islet; similar lesions can occur with aging.
    • Congo red is the classic confirmatory stain for amyloid deposits.

    Pancreatic Neuroendocrine Tumor — Pannet

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    BehaviorNeoplastic; biologic aggressiveness varies
    GrossUsually solitary, well-circumscribed pink-tan soft mass; larger lesions may hemorrhage/cystically degenerate
    MicroscopyUniform cells in nests, cords, ribbons, glands/festoons with coarsely stippled salt-and-pepper nuclei
    Confirming markerChromogranin A + synaptophysin
    Mimic killerPancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma → infiltrative malignant glands in desmoplastic stroma, not organoid neuroendocrine growth
    Tumor marker/labHormone depends on functioning subtype
    Malignant criterionInvasion/metastasis and proliferative grade/stage determine aggressive behavior; bland cytology alone does not prove benignity
    • Rubin describes PanNETs as circumscribed pink-tan masses composed of uniform cells in organoid nests/cords/glands and confirms endocrine differentiation with chromogranin A and synaptophysin.

    Insulinoma

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossUsually small, solitary, well-circumscribed PanNET
    MicroscopyNeuroendocrine nests/cords surrounding rich capillary networks ± stromal amyloid
    Confirming markerChromogranin/synaptophysin; insulin IHC may support functional phenotype
    Mimic killerExogenous insulin → C-peptide suppressed, unlike endogenous insulinoma SUPPLEMENTARY
    LabInappropriately high insulin during hypoglycemia; C-peptide ↑ with endogenous secretion
    Pathognomonic?Histologic appearance does not uniquely identify an insulinoma; the biochemical syndrome does

    Gastrinoma / Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome

    Rapid-review elementFinding
    GrossPancreatic, duodenal or peripancreatic neuroendocrine tumor; may be multiple
    MicroscopyTypical PanNET organoid architecture with neuroendocrine chromatin
    Confirming markerChromogranin/synaptophysin; gastrin IHC may support phenotype
    Mimic killerPPI-related hypergastrinemia → gastrin elevation without a gastrinoma mass/clinical tumor syndrome
    LabFasting gastrin markedly ↑ with gastric acid hypersecretion
    Clinical clueMultiple/refractory peptic ulcers + diarrhea
    Pathognomonic?Tumor morphology is not uniquely gastrinoma-specific; hormone/clinical correlation is required
    • Robbins links gastrinoma to marked gastrin hypersecretion, gastric acid excess and severe peptic ulceration and notes that gastrinomas may arise in pancreas, duodenum or peripancreatic tissue.

    ▪Benign vs Malignant — Endocrine Board Rules

    Lesion pairBenign / lower-risk criterionMalignant discriminator
    Follicular adenoma vs follicular carcinomaComplete capsule; no vascular invasionCapsular and/or vascular invasion
    Parathyroid adenoma vs carcinomaCircumscribed; no destructive invasionLocal invasion or metastasis
    Adrenocortical adenoma vs carcinomaSmall, homogeneous, low mitotic activity, no necrosis/invasionInvasion/metastasis + high mitoses/necrosis/aggressive morphology
    PheochromocytomaBland or bizarre cytology can still behave indolentlyMetastasis is definitive malignant behavior in classic pathology approach
    PanNETBland organoid morphology may still be neoplasticInvasion/metastasis + proliferative grade
    Pituitary tumorMost remain locally confinedDestructive invasion suggests aggressive behavior; metastasis defines carcinoma
    Papillary thyroid CAAlready malignant by diagnostic tumor morphologyNodal/distant spread determines stage
    Medullary thyroid CAN/AInvasive C-cell carcinoma; nodal/distant spread determines stage
    Anaplastic thyroid CAN/AIntrinsically high-grade malignant
    • Core rule: endocrine cytologic atypia ≠ malignancy.
    • Parathyroid: bizarre nuclei can occur in benign adenoma.
    • Adrenal cortex: mild pleomorphism may occur in benign adenoma.
    • Pheochromocytoma: striking pleomorphism can occur in tumors that remain confined to the adrenal.

    ▪Pathognomonic vs Characteristic — Board Safety

    FindingStrengthCorrect interpretation
    Pituitary reticulin lossHIGHLY CHARACTERISTICAdenoma/PitNET in correct sellar setting
    Postpartum anterior-pituitary infarctionDIAGNOSTIC CONTEXTSheehan syndrome
    Scalloped colloidCHARACTERISTICGraves
    Hürthle cellsNOT PATHOGNOMONICHashimoto and other thyroid lesions
    Orphan Annie nuclei/grooves/pseudoinclusionsDIAGNOSTIC MORPHOLOGIC PATTERNPapillary thyroid carcinoma
    Psammoma bodiesNOT PATHOGNOMONICSupport PTC but occur elsewhere
    Capsular/vascular invasionDECISIVE MALIGNANT CRITERIONFollicular carcinoma
    Amyloid + calcitoninHIGHLY DIAGNOSTICMedullary thyroid carcinoma
    Parathyroid endocrine atypiaNOT MALIGNANT BY ITSELFAdenomas can appear atypical
    Parathyroid invasion/metastasisDEFINITIVE MALIGNANT CRITERIONParathyroid carcinoma
    Crooke hyaline changeCHARACTERISTICHypercortisolism; does not identify source
    Spironolactone bodiesCHARACTERISTICTreated aldosterone-producing adenoma
    ZellballenHIGHLY CHARACTERISTICPheochromocytoma/paraganglioma
    Pheochromocytoma pleomorphismNOT A RELIABLE MALIGNANCY CRITERIONMetastasis is decisive
    InsulitisHIGHLY CHARACTERISTICActive T1DM
    Islet amyloidCHARACTERISTIC, NOT SPECIFICT2DM; also aging
    Salt-and-pepper chromatinSUGGESTIVE OF NEUROENDOCRINE DIFFERENTIATIONNot organ-specific

    ▪Final 20-Second Board Recall

    • Pituitary adenoma: monomorphic cells + absent/disrupted reticulin; functioning tumor = hormone excess, macroadenoma = mass effect.
    • Sheehan: postpartum hemorrhage → anterior-pituitary infarction → failure to lactate/panhypopituitarism.
    • Central DI: dilute polyuria + serum hyperosmolality; central disease responds to desmopressin.
    • Graves: diffuse meaty goiter + scalloped colloid + papillary infoldings without fibrovascular cores → T4/T3↑, TSH↓.
    • Hashimoto: Hürthle cells + lymphoid follicles/germinal centers → TSH↑, anti-TPO+.
    • Papillary thyroid CA: Orphan Annie nuclei + grooves + pseudoinclusions ± psammoma bodies → lymphatic spread.
    • Follicular thyroid CA: capsular/vascular invasion = carcinoma; FNA cannot make this distinction.
    • Medullary thyroid CA: calcitonin + Congo-red-positive amyloid + salt-and-pepper neuroendocrine cells.
    • Anaplastic thyroid CA: elderly patient + rapidly enlarging invasive neck mass + bizarre spindle/giant cells.
    • Parathyroid adenoma: single tan-red gland + chief cells + compressed normal rim → PTH↑, Ca↑, phosphate↓.
    • Parathyroid hyperplasia: multiple glands enlarged.
    • Parathyroid carcinoma: invasion/metastasis—not endocrine atypia—proves malignancy.
    • Cushing: ACTH excess → bilateral adrenal hyperplasia; exogenous steroids → bilateral cortical atrophy; Crooke change signals glucocorticoid excess.
    • Conn: small yellow cortical adenoma + aldosterone↑/renin↓ ± hypokalemic alkalosis.
    • Addison: small shrunken autoimmune adrenals + cortical destruction + preserved medulla → cortisol↓/ACTH↑ + hyperpigmentation.
    • Pheochromocytoma: zellballen + chromogranin/synaptophysin + S-100 sustentacular cells → metanephrines↑; pleomorphism does not prove malignancy.
    • Adrenal adenoma: small homogeneous yellow; adrenal carcinoma: large invasive variegated hemorrhagic-necrotic tumor.
    • T1DM: insulitis + β-cell loss; T2DM: islet amyloid, but amyloid is not specific.
    • PanNET: organoid nests/cords + salt-and-pepper nuclei + chromogranin/synaptophysin.
    • Insulinoma: hypoglycemia with inappropriate endogenous insulin elevation.
    • Gastrinoma: fasting gastrin↑ + multiple/refractory peptic ulcers + diarrhea.
    • Final malignancy rule: IHC establishes endocrine lineage; invasion/metastasis or disease-specific invasive criteria establish malignant behavior. Hormone excess establishes functionality—not malignancy.
    Plate XI — Endocrine DisordersPathology Rapid Review · PLE Board
    🧠XII
    Regional block · Primary blueprint: The PRC Board of Medicine Pathology TOS assigns 9% = 9 items to Cerebrovascular…

    Nervous System Disorders

    9% of Pathology PLE

    ▪Cerebrovascular Disease

    Ischemic Cerebral Infarction

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Ischemic cerebral infarction follows critical reduction or cessation of regional cerebral blood flow, most often from arterial thrombosis or embolism; failure of oxidative phosphorylation rapidly depletes ATP and disrupts ion pumps.
    • DΦMembrane depolarization → glutamate release and excitotoxic signaling → Ca²⁺ influx → activation of proteases, phospholipases, endonucleases, mitochondrial dysfunction, free-radical formation, and irreversible neuronal injury.
    • Neurons are particularly vulnerable and develop irreversible eosinophilic injury before glia; subsequent tissue necrosis recruits neutrophils followed by macrophages/microglia.
    • DPUnlike most solid-organ infarcts, ischemic brain undergoes liquefactive necrosis → macrophage digestion → cystic cavitation surrounded by reactive gliosis.
    Gross morphology
    • During the first several hours, the brain may show little gross change despite irreversible neuronal injury.
    • Over the next several days, the involved territory becomes swollen, soft and increasingly pale, with loss of distinction between gray and white matter.
    • During the subacute period the tissue becomes gelatinous and friable as liquefaction and macrophage removal progress.
    • Remote infarcts form irregular cystic cavities bordered by gliotic brain; the surface and cut section depend on the involved arterial territory rather than on a specific fixed size.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Within approximately the first day, damaged neurons become shrunken and intensely eosinophilic with pyknotic nuclei—the classic “red neuron”.
    • Neutrophils appear during the acute phase; by 48–72 hours, macrophages/microglia become prominent and eventually dominate as they ingest lipid-rich myelin debris.
    • Reactive astrocytes and neovascularization appear at the lesion edge during the following week; months later, a cystic cavity is surrounded by dense fibrillary gliosis.
    • No specific IHC proves infarction; GFAP highlights reactive astrocytes and CD68 SUPPLEMENTARY macrophages, but diagnosis rests on temporal morphology plus vascular distribution.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DPDestruction of a specific arterial territory → abrupt focal neurologic loss; for example, motor-cortex/internal-capsule infarction produces contralateral weakness because corticospinal neurons/axons are destroyed.
    • DPLarge infarctions → cytotoxic plus vasogenic edema → ICP ↑ → herniation risk, often peaking several days after stroke.
    • CT/MRI provides localization; diffusion-weighted MRI demonstrates acute ischemic injury before mature gross cavitation develops.
    • DPClosest mimic: necrotic brain tumor; infarction respects a vascular territory and evolves through red neurons → macrophages → cystic gliosis, while tumor contains a persistent proliferative neoplastic population.

    Infarct Timing — PLE Pattern

    Approximate phaseDominant morphology
    Early acuteRed neurons, edema
    1–2 daysNeutrophils increasingly evident
    2–3 days onwardMacrophages/microglia
    ~1 week onwardReactive astrocytes + neovascularization
    Weeks–monthsLiquefaction → cystic cavity + gliosis
    • Robbins specifically demonstrates eosinophilic acute neuronal injury, macrophage-rich subacute infarction, and the remote cystic gliotic infarct.

    Lacunar Infarcts / Hypertensive Small-Vessel Disease

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • DPLong-standing hypertension damages deep penetrating arterioles → hyaline arteriolosclerosis/small-vessel disease → progressive luminal narrowing and eventual thrombosis.
    • Complete occlusion of a deep penetrating artery causes a small infarct that undergoes liquefactive necrosis and forms a lacune.
    • The highest-yield sites are putamen, globus pallidus, thalamus, internal capsule, deep white matter, caudate and pons.
    • The same hypertensive arteriolar disease can either occlude vessels and cause lacunes or weaken them sufficiently to rupture and cause deep intracerebral hemorrhage.
    Gross morphology
    • Lacunes are small, round or irregular cavitary lesions, classically <15 mm, within deep gray nuclei, white matter, or brainstem.
    • They appear as small fluid-filled “lake-like” spaces rather than large wedge-shaped cortical infarcts.
    • Multiple old lacunes may create a perforated appearance in affected deep structures.
    • Classic gross descriptor: “small cavitary/lacunar infarct” in the basal ganglia/internal capsule/pons of a hypertensive patient.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Established lacunes consist of tissue loss surrounded by gliosis.
    • Adjacent small arteries/arterioles show thickened hyalinized walls and narrowed lumina.
    • Old lesions contain few residual macrophages, with astrocytic gliosis forming the permanent margin.
    • No disease-specific stain is required; PAS SUPPLEMENTARY can accentuate hyaline arteriolar wall thickening.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦInternal-capsule lacune → pure motor hemiparesis because densely packed corticospinal fibers are interrupted.
    • DΦThalamic lacune → pure sensory stroke through destruction of sensory relay nuclei/tracts.
    • Multiple cumulative lacunes contribute to gait disturbance, pseudobulbar dysfunction and vascular cognitive impairment.
    • Closest mimic: large-artery thromboembolic infarction; lacunes are small deep cavities supplied by penetrating vessels, not cortical territorial infarcts.

    Spontaneous Intraparenchymal Hemorrhage

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Hypertension is the major cause of deep intraparenchymal hemorrhage; chronic pressure injury causes hyaline arteriolosclerosis and other vessel-wall abnormalities that weaken penetrating vessels.
    • Deep hypertensive hemorrhage classically involves putamen/basal ganglia, thalamus, pons and cerebellum.
    • DΦVessel rupture → rapidly expanding hematoma → direct tissue destruction + mass effect + surrounding edema → increased ICP and possible ventricular rupture.
    • Cerebral amyloid angiopathy (CAA) instead causes amyloid deposition in cortical/leptomeningeal vessel walls and is a major cause of lobar hemorrhage in older adults.
    Gross morphology
    • Acute hypertensive hemorrhage forms a deep red hematoma, often centered in the putamen or thalamus and sometimes rupturing into a lateral ventricle.
    • Large hematomas distort adjacent structures and may generate marked hemispheric swelling and herniation.
    • CAA produces more superficial lobar hemorrhages involving cortex/subcortical white matter, sometimes dissecting into the subarachnoid space.
    • Remote hemorrhage evolves into a cavity with a brown hemosiderin-stained rim.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Acute lesions show extensive extravasated erythrocytes, disrupted neural tissue and surrounding edema.
    • Over time, macrophages remove erythrocytes and become hemosiderin- and lipid-laden; reactive astrocytes proliferate around the lesion.
    • Hypertensive vessels show hyaline arteriolosclerosis; CAA vessels show amyloid within cortical/leptomeningeal vessel walls.
    • Congo red SUPPLEMENTARY or Aβ immunohistochemistry highlights vascular amyloid in CAA.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦPutaminal/internal-capsule hemorrhage → contralateral motor deficit because the expanding hematoma disrupts corticospinal fibers.
    • Pontine hemorrhage may abruptly impair consciousness and vital brainstem function; ventricular extension predicts severe clinical deterioration.
    • CAA should be considered in an older patient with recurrent lobar, rather than deep ganglionic, hemorrhages.
    • Closest mimic: hemorrhagic transformation of ischemic infarction; primary hypertensive hemorrhage is centered on a ruptured deep vessel, while hemorrhagic infarction follows a pre-existing ischemic vascular territory.

    Deep vs Lobar Hemorrhage

    PatternPrototype causeMorphologic clue
    Deep ganglionicChronic hypertensionHyaline small-vessel disease
    Lobar cortical/subcorticalCAAAβ in vessel walls
    Hemorrhagic infarctReperfusion/venous infarctionBlood within an ischemic territory

    Saccular (“BERRY”) Aneurysm and Subarachnoid Hemorrhage

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • A saccular aneurysm is a thin-walled arterial outpouching that develops preferentially at branch points of the circle of Willis, particularly in the anterior circulation.
    • The aneurysmal wall lacks normal structural support, including normal smooth muscle and internal elastic components, so hemodynamic stress promotes progressive expansion.
    • Hypertension and cigarette smoking increase rupture risk; associations include ADPKD and selected connective-tissue disorders.
    • DPRupture releases arterial blood under pressure into the subarachnoid space → abrupt rise in ICP; secondary vasospasm can later produce cerebral ischemia/infarction.
    Gross morphology
    • Unruptured aneurysm is a bright-red, shiny, thin translucent sac projecting from an arterial branch point; sizes range from millimeters to several centimeters.
    • After rupture, blood coats the basal cisterns and subarachnoid surfaces and tracks along cerebral sulci.
    • Large SAH can obscure vessels at the brain base and may extend into ventricles or parenchyma.
    • Classic gross descriptor: berry-like saccular outpouching of the circle of Willis with diffuse basal subarachnoid blood.
    Microscopic morphology
    • The aneurysmal sac has a thin fibrous/hyalinized wall with marked reduction or absence of normal media and elastic lamina.
    • Thrombus and organization may occur within unruptured or partially thrombosed aneurysms.
    • Rupture produces erythrocytes within the leptomeningeal/subarachnoid compartment, later followed by macrophages containing hemosiderin.
    • Elastic stain SUPPLEMENTARY can demonstrate disruption/loss of elastic tissue but is not needed to diagnose a typical saccular aneurysm.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • Sudden rupture classically produces a “worst headache of life”, vomiting, meningismus and rapid loss of consciousness because arterial blood abruptly raises ICP and irritates meninges.
    • CT detects acute subarachnoid blood; CSF xanthochromia SUPPLEMENTARY supports SAH when imaging is nondiagnostic after an appropriate interval.
    • Delayed vasospasm can cause secondary infarction; rebleeding and hydrocephalus are additional major complications.
    • Closest mimic: traumatic SAH; a spontaneous basal SAH with an identifiable circle-of-Willis aneurysm favors saccular aneurysm, while trauma usually accompanies other traumatic parenchymal lesions.

    ▪Cerebral Edema, Hydrocephalus, ICP and Herniation

    Cerebral Edema

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Cerebral edema is an abnormal increase in brain water; because the rigid cranium cannot expand appreciably, increasing tissue volume directly raises intracranial pressure.
    • DΦVasogenic edema follows blood-brain barrier disruption → extracellular accumulation of fluid, particularly in white matter; tumors, abscesses, hemorrhage and meningitis are major settings.
    • DPCytotoxic edema follows cellular energy/ionic failure with an intact barrier → intracellular swelling of neurons, glia and endothelial cells; ischemia is a major prototype.
    • Interstitial edema occurs with hydrocephalus as CSF traverses the ependyma and accumulates in periventricular white matter.
    Gross morphology
    • Diffuse severe edema produces an increased-weight, swollen brain.
    • Gyri become broad and flattened, while sulci narrow or disappear as the swollen brain presses against the skull.
    • Ventricles are compressed in generalized edema unless hydrocephalus is the cause.
    • Classic gross descriptor: flattened gyri + narrowed sulci = cerebral swelling/edema.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Vasogenic edema produces widened extracellular spaces, most prominent within white matter.
    • Cytotoxic edema produces cellular swelling that may be subtle on routine H&E until severe ischemic injury develops.
    • Interstitial edema produces periventricular white-matter pallor/vacuolation from transependymal CSF passage.
    • No specific stain confirms cerebral edema; imaging plus underlying lesion morphology establishes the mechanism.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DPExpanding brain volume → headache, vomiting, papilledema and impaired consciousness through increased ICP and reduced perfusion.
    • Focal vasogenic edema around a tumor/abscess amplifies mass effect beyond the actual lesion dimensions.
    • DPSevere generalized edema → pressure gradients → herniation, vascular compression and secondary infarction/hemorrhage.
    • Closest mimic: hydrocephalus; generalized edema tends to compress ventricles, whereas hydrocephalus dilates them.

    Hydrocephalus

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Hydrocephalus is accumulation of CSF within the ventricular system causing ventricular dilation.
    • Noncommunicating/obstructive hydrocephalus results from an intraventricular blockage; ventricles proximal to the obstruction dilate while downstream compartments remain relatively normal.
    • The narrow cerebral aqueduct is a common obstructive site; tumors near the fourth ventricle, aqueduct, or foramen of Monro can produce the same physiology.
    • Communicating hydrocephalus reflects impaired CSF resorption after the CSF has exited the ventricles, commonly following meningitis or subarachnoid hemorrhage.
    Gross morphology
    • The dominant finding is ventriculomegaly with thinning/stretching of adjacent white matter.
    • Obstructive disease produces asymmetric/selective enlargement determined by the level of obstruction.
    • Longstanding severe hydrocephalus causes marked thinning of cerebral mantle, particularly in young patients with incompletely fused cranial sutures.
    • Classic gross distinction: proximal-only ventricular dilation = noncommunicating; generalized ventricular dilation = communicating.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Periventricular white matter shows interstitial edema caused by transependymal CSF flow.
    • Ependymal lining may become stretched or disrupted in longstanding severe ventricular enlargement.
    • Chronic pressure causes white-matter rarefaction and axonal injury.
    • No special stain identifies hydrocephalus; imaging directly demonstrates the ventricular pattern.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦVentricular expansion increases ICP → headache, vomiting, papilledema and altered consciousness when compensation is exhausted.
    • A fourth-ventricular ependymoma or cerebellar medulloblastoma can obstruct CSF flow and produce symptomatic hydrocephalus.
    • Postmeningitic leptomeningeal fibrosis impairs CSF resorption and can cause communicating hydrocephalus.
    • Closest mimic: hydrocephalus ex vacuo; ex vacuo ventricular enlargement accompanies loss of brain volume without raised CSF pressure, as in advanced Alzheimer disease.

    Increased Intracranial Pressure and Brain Herniation

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Increased ICP results when expanding brain, blood, CSF, or tumor volume exceeds intracranial compensatory capacity.
    • Pressure gradients displace brain beneath rigid dural folds or through the foramen magnum, producing subfalcine, transtentorial/uncal, or tonsillar herniation.
    • Herniation injures neurons directly through compression and indirectly by compressing arteries/veins, producing infarction, edema and hemorrhage.
    • Progressive brainstem distortion can tear small penetrating vessels, producing secondary Duret hemorrhages SUPPLEMENTARY, and eventually disrupt respiratory/cardiovascular centers.
    Gross morphology
    • Subfalcine herniation: cingulate gyrus is displaced beneath the falx; compression of the anterior cerebral artery may produce medial frontal infarction.
    • Uncal/transtentorial herniation: medial temporal lobe/uncus is displaced through the tentorial notch; the third cranial nerve and posterior cerebral artery are particularly vulnerable.
    • Tonsillar herniation: cerebellar tonsils are forced through the foramen magnum and compress the medulla.
    • Classic gross warning: grooving/notching of displaced brain structures signals substantial sustained pressure and impending brainstem injury.
    Microscopic morphology
    • No primary pathognomonic histology exists; findings reflect secondary ischemia, hemorrhage and necrosis.
    • PCA compression during uncal herniation may produce occipital ischemic infarction.
    • Brainstem compression may show petechial/linear hemorrhages and neuronal ischemic injury.
    • GFAP later highlights reactive gliosis around surviving margins but does not establish herniation.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DPUncal compression of CN III → ipsilateral dilated poorly reactive pupil before global brainstem failure.
    • Corticospinal compression produces contralateral weakness; advanced brainstem compression causes declining consciousness and abnormal respiratory patterns.
    • DΦTonsillar herniation → medullary compression → abrupt respiratory/cardiovascular arrest.
    • Closest mimic: uncomplicated cerebral edema; herniation requires actual displacement across a dural compartment or foramen, not swelling alone.

    ▪CNS Infections

    Acute Pyogenic Bacterial Meningitis

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • DΦBacteria gain access hematogenously, by direct extension, or by implantation → proliferate in CSF/leptomeninges → intense neutrophilic response.
    • Common pathogens vary with age; Robbins highlights group B streptococci/E. coli in neonates, N. meningitidis in adolescents/young adults, and S. pneumoniae/Listeria in older adults.
    • Neutrophilic exudate spreads through the subarachnoid space and around leptomeningeal vessels; severe inflammation can extend into brain as cerebritis.
    • Vasculitis and venous thrombosis can produce secondary hemorrhagic infarction, while chronic arachnoid fibrosis can obstruct CSF resorption and cause hydrocephalus.
    Gross morphology
    • Leptomeninges become congested and covered by cloudy to frankly purulent exudate.
    • Purulent material fills sulci and follows the distribution of the subarachnoid space.
    • Brain may be swollen because of associated edema.
    • Classic gross descriptor: purulent leptomeningeal exudate coating cerebral convexities/sulci.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Neutrophils fill the subarachnoid space; in milder disease they cluster around leptomeningeal vessels.
    • Fulminant disease may show inflammatory invasion of vessel walls, thrombosis and extension into adjacent brain.
    • Gram stain can demonstrate organisms in untreated cases; culture/molecular studies identify the species.
    • Mimic killer: viral meningitis has predominantly lymphocytic pleocytosis, much less suppurative leptomeningeal inflammation and usually normal CSF glucose.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦMeningeal inflammation → headache, photophobia and neck stiffness; systemic cytokine response produces fever and constitutional symptoms.
    • DΦCSF is cloudy/purulent with opening pressure ↑, neutrophils ↑↑, protein ↑, glucose ↓.
    • Complications include cerebral edema, hydrocephalus, vasculitic infarcts, venous thrombosis, seizures and cranial nerve injury.
    • Closest mimic: viral meningitis—neutrophils + low glucose strongly favor pyogenic meningitis; lymphocytes + near-normal glucose favor viral disease.

    Brain Abscess

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Brain abscess is a localized focus of suppurative liquefactive necrosis caused most commonly by pyogenic infection.
    • Organisms reach brain by contiguous spread from sinus/middle-ear/mastoid infection, hematogenous dissemination, or direct implantation after trauma/surgery.
    • DΦNeutrophilic tissue destruction → central liquefaction; surrounding macrophage-mediated cleanup and vascular granulation tissue organize the lesion.
    • Newly formed capillaries are highly permeable, producing marked vasogenic edema; later fibroblasts from vessel walls form a collagenous capsule.
    Gross morphology
    • Abscess is a discrete cavity with a soft yellow-green necrotic/purulent center surrounded by a hyperemic rim.
    • Surrounding white matter is swollen from vasogenic edema.
    • Hematogenous spread can produce multiple lesions, often near gray-white junctions SUPPLEMENTARY.
    • Mature abscess develops a firm capsule separating the liquefied center from surrounding brain.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Central region contains liquefactive necrosis, neutrophils and cellular debris.
    • The outer margin contains proliferating capillaries and granulation tissue.
    • Mature lesions develop a collagenous capsule with an external rim of reactive gemistocytic astrocytes/gliosis.
    • Gram, fungal or other organism-specific stains are chosen according to suspected cause; culture supplies etiologic confirmation.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦMass effect + edema → headache, focal neurologic deficits, seizures and ICP elevation; fever may or may not dominate.
    • Imaging typically shows an enhancing cavity with surrounding edema; blood cultures may reveal the source.
    • Lumbar puncture may be hazardous with marked mass effect and may yield nonspecific findings because the lesion is parenchymal rather than leptomeningeal.
    • Closest mimic: glioblastoma; abscess contains neutrophilic liquefactive pus + organizing capsule, while glioblastoma has viable malignant astrocytic cells around pseudopalisading necrosis.

    Herpes Simplex Virus Type 1 Encephalitis

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • HSV-1 infection produces a necrotizing viral encephalitis that preferentially targets the inferior/medial temporal lobes and orbital frontal regions.
    • Viral replication causes neuronal and glial death; immune responses add perivascular lymphocytic inflammation and microglial activation.
    • Destruction is often hemorrhagic, distinguishing severe HSV encephalitis morphologically from many other viral encephalitides.
    • Neuronal injury produces neuronophagia and microglial nodules, while infected neurons and glia may develop intranuclear viral inclusions.
    Gross morphology
    • Temporal and inferior frontal lobes are swollen, soft and hemorrhagic, with irregular areas of necrosis.
    • Distribution is often asymmetric but characteristically involves limbic/temporal structures.
    • Severe lesions can show extensive destruction of anterior/inferomedial temporal tissue.
    • Classic gross descriptor: hemorrhagic necrotizing temporal-lobe encephalitis.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Perivascular lymphocytic cuffs and microglial nodules are classic viral-encephalitis patterns.
    • Necrotic neurons undergo neuronophagia.
    • Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions may be present in neurons and glia.
    • HSV immunostaining can highlight viral antigen; in clinical diagnosis CSF HSV PCR is the key confirmatory test.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦTemporal/limbic injury → altered behavior, personality, memory and consciousness; seizures are common.
    • CSF shows lymphocytic pleocytosis and increased protein; RBCs may appear because the process is hemorrhagic.
    • CSF HSV PCR SUPPLEMENTARY is highly useful early; tissue IHC supports diagnosis when biopsy/autopsy material is available.
    • Closest mimic: autoimmune limbic encephalitis; HSV favors hemorrhagic necrosis + viral inclusions/PCR positivity.

    Tuberculous Meningitis / CNS Tuberculosis

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • CNS tuberculosis usually results from hematogenous dissemination from an extracranial focus, classically pulmonary infection.
    • DΦMycobacteria localize in meninges or brain → delayed hypersensitivity and macrophage activation → granulomatous inflammation and caseous necrosis.
    • Basal meningeal inflammation can encase cranial nerves and arteries, causing neuropathies and vasculitic infarction.
    • DΦFibrosis around basal cisterns may obstruct CSF pathways → communicating hydrocephalus.
    Gross morphology
    • Thick gelatinous/fibrinous exudate at the base of the brain is a high-yield pattern.
    • Meninges may contain small tubercles/granulomas; parenchymal tuberculomas form firm nodular lesions with central caseation.
    • Hydrocephalus and secondary infarcts may accompany basal meningeal disease.
    • Classic gross descriptor: basal meningitis with caseating tubercles.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Meninges show granulomas composed of epithelioid histiocytes and Langhans-type giant cells, often with central caseous necrosis.
    • Lymphocytes surround granulomas; vessel walls may show granulomatous arteritis.
    • Ziehl-Neelsen/acid-fast stain may demonstrate organisms, but sensitivity can be limited.
    • Mimic killer: fungal granulomatous meningitis; organism-specific stains/culture/PCR distinguish fungi from acid-fast mycobacteria.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DPBasal meningeal irritation → headache, neck stiffness and cranial neuropathies; arterial involvement can produce focal deficits from infarction.
    • DΦCSF typically shows lymphocytic pleocytosis, protein ↑ and glucose ↓; microbiologic testing establishes etiology.
    • DPFibrotic obstruction of CSF resorption → hydrocephalus → raised ICP.
    • Closest mimic: viral meningitis; TB generally has low CSF glucose + high protein + chronic granulomatous morphology, unlike uncomplicated viral meningitis.

    ▪Demyelinating Disease

    Multiple Sclerosis

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Multiple sclerosis (MS) is an immune-mediated demyelinating disease in which T-cell and macrophage responses target CNS myelin and oligodendrocytes.
    • Active inflammation strips previously normal myelin while axons are initially relatively preserved, explaining impaired conduction with potential early recovery.
    • Recurrent attacks produce repeated demyelination; over time, secondary axonal loss becomes substantial and drives irreversible neurologic disability.
    • Lesions occur at different ages in the same patient, creating the clinical and pathologic principle of dissemination in space and time.
    Gross morphology
    • Brain and spinal cord show multiple sharply demarcated gray-tan plaques in white matter.
    • High-yield locations include periventricular white matter, optic nerves/chiasm, brainstem, cerebellar peduncles and spinal cord.
    • Chronic plaques become firm and gray because myelin has been lost and replaced by gliosis.
    • Classic gross descriptor: multiple periventricular demyelinating plaques.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Active plaques are highly cellular because of abundant lipid-laden macrophages containing myelin debris plus perivascular lymphocytes.
    • Luxol fast blue-PAS shows striking loss of myelin in active plaques.
    • Neurofilament immunostain demonstrates relative preservation of axons early; inactive plaques show severe myelin loss, reduced oligodendrocytes and prominent gliosis.
    • Shadow plaques contain thin residual/remyelinated sheaths and represent partial remyelination rather than normal white matter.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦOptic-nerve demyelination → optic neuritis/visual loss; spinal-cord plaque → weakness, sensory deficit or sphincter dysfunction; brainstem plaque can produce diplopia.
    • CSF often shows oligoclonal IgG bands and increased IgG index SUPPLEMENTARY.
    • MRI demonstrates lesions separated anatomically and temporally, paralleling the underlying multifocal plaque morphology.
    • Closest mimic: neuromyelitis optica; NMO strongly favors optic nerve/spinal cord disease with AQP4 antibodies and more destructive lesions containing neutrophils/eosinophils.

    ▪Neurodegenerative Disease

    Alzheimer Disease

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Alzheimer disease (AD) is characterized by progressive neuronal dysfunction and loss associated with accumulation of extracellular Aβ and intracellular abnormal tau.
    • Aβ deposition triggers synaptic dysfunction and activates microglia/astrocytes; tau becomes abnormally phosphorylated, destabilizing microtubules and aggregating into neurofibrillary tangles.
    • Neuronal death and synapse loss are particularly severe in hippocampal/medial temporal structures and association cortices.
    • DΦProgressive tissue loss → cortical atrophy → secondary ventricular enlargement, which represents hydrocephalus ex vacuo, not raised-pressure hydrocephalus.
    Gross morphology
    • Brain shows cortical atrophy with narrowed gyri and widened sulci, particularly in frontal, temporal and parietal lobes.
    • Hippocampus, entorhinal cortex and amygdala are involved early and become markedly atrophic with progression.
    • Lateral ventricles enlarge secondarily because brain volume has been lost.
    • Classic gross descriptor: diffuse cortical atrophy + widened sulci + hydrocephalus ex vacuo.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Neuritic plaques consist of dystrophic neurites surrounding an extracellular Aβ-rich amyloid core with nearby reactive microglia/astrocytes.
    • Neurofibrillary tangles are intraneuronal aggregates of hyperphosphorylated tau.
    • Congo red or Aβ immunohistochemistry identifies plaque amyloid; phospho-tau IHC highlights tangles.
    • Neither plaques nor tangles in small numbers are individually pathognomonic because they can increase with age; diagnostic significance depends on burden and distribution.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DPHippocampal/entorhinal degeneration → progressive recent-memory impairment; association-cortex loss subsequently causes language, executive and visuospatial dysfunction.
    • DΦCSF biomarkers can show Aβ ↓ and phosphorylated tau ↑; Robbins notes such biomarkers can reveal AD-related pathology before overt dementia.
    • Progressive cortical loss leads to profound dementia, dependency and eventual complications of immobility/dysphagia.
    • Closest mimic: dementia with Lewy bodies; DLB favors widespread α-synuclein Lewy bodies, fluctuating cognition and visual hallucinations, rather than dominant plaque/tangle pathology.

    Parkinson Disease

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Parkinson disease (PD) results from progressive loss of dopaminergic pigmented neurons of the substantia nigra pars compacta.
    • Reduced nigrostriatal dopamine disturbs basal-ganglia motor circuitry, producing bradykinesia, rigidity and resting tremor.
    • Surviving neurons commonly accumulate α-synuclein aggregates forming Lewy bodies and Lewy neurites.
    • Mitochondrial dysfunction, impaired protein clearance and abnormal α-synuclein handling contribute to selective neuronal degeneration.
    Gross morphology
    • The classic gross feature is pallor/depigmentation of the substantia nigra.
    • The locus ceruleus can also lose its normal pigment.
    • Overall brain weight may initially be near normal because the striking lesion is selective rather than diffuse cortical atrophy.
    • Classic gross descriptor: pale substantia nigra.
    Microscopic morphology
    • There is loss of pigmented substantia nigra neurons with reactive gliosis.
    • Lewy bodies are round/elongated eosinophilic intracytoplasmic neuronal inclusions with a dense core and pale halo.
    • α-Synuclein immunohistochemistry highlights Lewy bodies and Lewy neurites.
    • Lewy bodies are highly characteristic of synucleinopathy but are not exclusive to idiopathic PD because they also occur in dementia with Lewy bodies.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦNigrostriatal neuron loss → bradykinesia, resting tremor, rigidity and postural instability.
    • Spread of synuclein pathology to autonomic and cortical systems can produce autonomic dysfunction and dementia in advanced disease.
    • There is no routine serum or CSF marker that independently confirms sporadic PD; diagnosis remains predominantly clinical.
    • Closest mimic: multiple-system atrophy; MSA shows α-synuclein–positive glial cytoplasmic inclusions predominantly in oligodendroglia, rather than classic neuronal Lewy-body predominance.

    Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD) is a prion disease produced by conformational conversion and propagation of abnormal prion protein.
    • Misfolded protein induces conformational alteration of normal PrP and accumulates despite proteolytic-clearance mechanisms.
    • Rapid neuronal dysfunction and cell loss produce a remarkably fast neurodegenerative course compared with Alzheimer or Parkinson disease.
    • CNS inflammation is minimal despite extensive injury, an important distinction from infectious encephalitis.
    Gross morphology
    • Brain may be grossly near normal early and may show nonspecific cerebral/cerebellar atrophy in advanced disease.
    • There is no purulent exudate, hemorrhagic temporal-lobe destruction, or mass-forming lesion.
    • The dominant abnormality is microscopic rather than gross.
    • Classic gross descriptor: none; rapid clinical course + spongiform microscopic change is the board pattern.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Gray matter develops spongiform change caused by multiple small intracellular vacuoles in neurons and glia.
    • Neuronal loss and reactive gliosis accompany the vacuolar change.
    • Amyloid-like prion plaques occur in selected prion-disease variants but are not mandatory in conventional CJD.
    • Prion-protein IHC can demonstrate abnormal deposits; RT-QuIC in CSF SUPPLEMENTARY provides highly useful clinical laboratory confirmation.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦRapid neuronal loss → rapidly progressive dementia with myoclonus, ataxia and neurologic decline over months rather than years.
    • CSF RT-QuIC SUPPLEMENTARY supports the diagnosis; 14-3-3 protein is less specific and should be interpreted cautiously.
    • EEG may show characteristic periodic abnormalities SUPPLEMENTARY, but morphology remains the defining pathologic feature.
    • Closest mimic: Alzheimer disease; CJD is much more rapid and shows spongiform vacuolation, not a dominant plaque-and-tangle pattern.

    ▪Primary CNS Tumors

    Diffuse Astrocytoma / Glioblastoma

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Diffuse astrocytic tumors infiltrate brain along existing tissue planes rather than forming a truly encapsulated mass.
    • Increasing grade corresponds to greater cellularity, atypia and mitotic activity; glioblastoma additionally shows necrosis and/or microvascular proliferation in the Robbins 10th-edition morphologic framework.
    • Hypoxic tumor cells near necrosis generate angiogenic signaling, contributing to exuberant endothelial/microvascular proliferation and highly permeable tumor vessels.
    • Leaky neoplastic vessels cause marked vasogenic edema and contrast enhancement; infiltrative cells extend beyond radiologically obvious tumor borders.
    Gross morphology
    • Lower-grade diffuse astrocytomas are poorly defined gray infiltrative masses that expand/distort brain and may be firm, soft, or gelatinous.
    • Glioblastoma is highly heterogeneous and variegated, with firm gray-white tumor intermixed with yellow necrosis and red hemorrhage.
    • Extensive infiltration may cross white-matter pathways; SUPPLEMENTARY a lesion crossing the corpus callosum can create a “butterfly” pattern.
    • Classic gross descriptor: necrotic, hemorrhagic infiltrating cerebral mass = glioblastoma.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Diffuse astrocytoma contains hypercellular infiltrating glial cells with irregular hyperchromatic nuclei in a fibrillary, often GFAP-positive background.
    • Glioblastoma shows serpiginous/pseudopalisading necrosis—hypercellular tumor cells line up around necrotic zones.
    • Microvascular proliferation produces glomeruloid/tufted proliferating vascular structures.
    • GFAP supports astrocytic differentiation; IDH1 R132H IHC can identify many IDH-mutant diffuse gliomas, but neither alone proves high-grade behavior.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦTumor infiltration and edema → headache, seizures, focal deficits and ICP elevation according to location.
    • Glioblastoma often shows irregular/ring-like enhancement because central necrosis is surrounded by viable tumor and leaky neovasculature.
    • No useful serum tumor marker replaces tissue diagnosis; grading depends on histology/molecular classification rather than TNM staging.
    • Closest mimic: brain abscess; glioblastoma has viable atypical GFAP-positive tumor cells + pseudopalisading necrosis/microvascular proliferation, whereas abscess has central pus with neutrophils and an organizing inflammatory capsule.

    Oligodendroglioma

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Oligodendroglioma is an infiltrating glioma composed of cells resembling oligodendrocytes and occurs predominantly in cerebral hemispheric white matter.
    • Tumor infiltrates cortex and white matter, sometimes extending around neurons as perineuronal satellitosis.
    • Low-grade tumors may progress to more cellular, mitotically active anaplastic forms.
    • Robbins identifies oligodendroglioma by integrated morphology plus IDH mutation and 1p/19q codeletion, which are retained here because they appear directly on diagnostic pathology reports.
    Gross morphology
    • Tumors are soft/gelatinous gray masses in cerebral hemispheres.
    • Cysts, focal hemorrhage and especially calcification are common.
    • Borders may appear relatively defined grossly but infiltrating tumor extends microscopically into surrounding brain.
    • Classic gross clue: calcified gelatinous cerebral hemispheric mass in an adult with seizures.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Cells have regular round nuclei surrounded by clear perinuclear halos, producing the classic “fried-egg” appearance.
    • Delicate branching thin-walled capillaries form the classic “chicken-wire” vasculature.
    • Calcification may be extensive; tumor cells may cluster around normal cortical neurons.
    • IDH1 R132H IHC may be positive; demonstration of 1p/19q codeletion distinguishes canonical oligodendroglioma from astrocytic mimics.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • Cortical involvement often produces seizures, sometimes for years before diagnosis.
    • DΦIncreasing size → focal deficits, headache and mass effect.
    • Molecular pathology report demonstrating IDH alteration + 1p/19q codeletion supplies important diagnostic classification.
    • Closest mimic: diffuse astrocytoma; oligodendroglioma favors round haloed nuclei + chicken-wire capillaries + 1p/19q codeletion, while astrocytoma has elongated irregular nuclei in a fibrillary GFAP-rich background.

    Ependymoma

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Ependymoma arises from ependymal-lineage cells adjacent to ventricles or the spinal central canal.
    • In children it classically arises near the fourth ventricle; tumor growth can obstruct CSF outflow and cause hydrocephalus.
    • Adult lesions more commonly involve spinal cord; tumor may disseminate through CSF.
    • The tumor is relatively circumscribed compared with diffuse astrocytoma, explaining why complete resection can sometimes be achieved.
    Gross morphology
    • Posterior-fossa ependymoma forms a relatively circumscribed mass within/filling the fourth ventricle.
    • Tumor can extend through lateral recesses/foramina into subarachnoid spaces.
    • Cut surface is gray-tan and may contain cystic or hemorrhagic change.
    • Classic gross clue: fourth-ventricular mass causing hydrocephalus in a child.
    Microscopic morphology
    • The principal hallmark is the perivascular pseudorosette: tumor-cell processes radiate toward a central blood vessel.
    • True ependymal rosettes, with cells surrounding a central lumen, may occur but are less frequent.
    • GFAP often highlights processes in pseudorosettes; EMA demonstrates characteristic dotlike/ringlike cytoplasmic staining.
    • Mimic killer: medulloblastoma shows densely packed primitive blue cells and Homer-Wright rosettes rather than ependymal perivascular pseudorosettes.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DPFourth-ventricle obstruction → hydrocephalus → headache, vomiting and papilledema.
    • Cerebellar/brainstem compression produces ataxia and cranial nerve deficits depending on extension.
    • CSF cytology may demonstrate disseminated tumor cells in cases with leptomeningeal spread.
    • Closest mimic: medulloblastoma—ependymoma is more circumscribed and EMA/GFAP-positive with perivascular pseudorosettes.

    Medulloblastoma

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Medulloblastoma is a highly malignant embryonal tumor occurring predominantly in children and, by definition in Robbins, arising in the cerebellum.
    • DΦRapid tumor growth can fill/compress the fourth ventricle → obstructive hydrocephalus.
    • Tumor cells readily enter subarachnoid spaces and disseminate through CSF.
    • Histologic and molecular subtypes vary prognostically, but morphology remains centered on a densely cellular embryonal small-blue-cell tumor.
    Gross morphology
    • In children tumors commonly arise in/near the cerebellar vermis and may protrude toward the fourth ventricle.
    • The tumor is soft, gray-tan and poorly differentiated, sometimes with hemorrhage or necrosis.
    • Mass effect can distort cerebellum and obstruct CSF pathways.
    • Classic gross clue: midline cerebellar mass in a child with hydrocephalus.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Tumor consists of sheets of densely packed primitive small blue cells with scant cytoplasm and high N:C ratio.
    • Homer-Wright rosettes consist of tumor cells arranged around central fibrillary neuropil; they occur in a substantial subset but are not mandatory.
    • Mitotic and apoptotic activity is high.
    • Synaptophysin SUPPLEMENTARY supports neuronal differentiation; reticulin highlights internodular zones in desmoplastic/nodular variants.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • DΦVermian/cerebellar destruction → truncal ataxia, gait instability and coordination deficits.
    • DΦFourth-ventricle compression → increased ICP with morning headache/vomiting and papilledema.
    • CSF spread produces surface “icing” or spinal “drop metastases,” particularly around the cauda equina.
    • Closest mimic: ependymoma; medulloblastoma shows small blue cells + Homer-Wright rosettes, whereas ependymoma shows perivascular pseudorosettes and EMA dot/ring staining.

    Meningioma

    Etiology and pathogenesis
    • Meningioma arises from meningothelial/arachnoid cells and is usually attached to dura.
    • Most are slowly growing and mechanically compress underlying brain rather than diffusely infiltrating it.
    • Increasing mitotic activity, brain invasion and selected histologic patterns correspond to higher WHO grade and greater recurrence risk.
    • Because the intracranial cavity is confined, even a histologically low-grade meningioma can cause substantial neurologic dysfunction through compression.
    Gross morphology
    • Most are well-circumscribed rubbery, rounded or bosselated dura-based masses that are easily separated from underlying brain.
    • Cut surface is tan and fleshy; heavily calcified tumors can feel gritty/sand-like.
    • Some spread as an en plaque sheet and can induce hyperostosis of adjacent skull.
    • Classic gross descriptor: dural-based extra-axial mass compressing rather than infiltrating brain.
    Microscopic morphology
    • Tumor cells commonly form concentric whorls.
    • Progressive calcification of whorls forms laminated psammoma bodies.
    • EMA is commonly positive; SSTR2A SUPPLEMENTARY is another useful meningothelial marker.
    • Mimic killer: schwannoma is diffusely S-100 positive and shows Antoni A/B areas and Verocay bodies, rather than meningothelial whorls/psammoma bodies.
    Clinicopathologic correlation, laboratory findings, complications, and prognosis
    • Convexity/parasagittal tumors commonly produce seizures by compressing adjacent cortex.
    • DPOlfactory-groove lesions → anosmia; suprasellar lesions → visual impairment; cerebellopontine-angle lesions → cranial nerve deficits.
    • There is no useful serum tumor marker; WHO histologic grade and resectability predict recurrence more directly than conventional TNM staging.
    • Benign vs malignant: most are grade 1; brain invasion/increased mitotic activity supports atypical grade 2 disease, while anaplastic morphology defines aggressive high-grade lesions in the cited classification.

    Tumor Behavior Rules

    • CNS tumors generally rely more heavily on WHO grade, molecular class, location and resectability than on conventional TNM staging.
    • A histologically low-grade tumor can be lethal if it obstructs CSF or compresses vital brain structures.
    • Primary CNS tumors rarely metastasize outside the CNS; CSF dissemination is especially important for medulloblastoma and can occur with ependymoma.
    • There is no routine serum tumor marker with adequate sensitivity/specificity to diagnose glioblastoma, oligodendroglioma, ependymoma, medulloblastoma or meningioma.
    • IHC identifies lineage; grade-defining morphology, invasion and biologic behavior determine aggressiveness.
